03Picking the right unit and quantitySix each is not six cases+

Your goal

Resolve a unit-of-measure misunderstanding, calculate the product-specific case equivalent, and confirm the requested total.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Six each is not six cases

Pick ticket S18 requests six each of a storage tray. For this item, one standard case contains six trays. Coworker Omar initially reads the request as six cases, which would contain thirty-six trays. Picker Asha clarifies that the requested six individual trays equal one standard case of this item. The correction does not establish the pack size of any other product, authorize a different quantity, or confirm that picking and dispatch have already been completed.

Omar: Warehouse coworker / Asha: Picker

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does S18 request?

Answer and explanation

A. Six individual trays

Six each means six individual trays, not six full cases.

2. What is the stated pack size for this item?

Answer and explanation

D. Six trays per standard case

The briefing defines one standard case as containing six trays for this item.

3. How many standard cases equal the requested quantity?

Answer and explanation

A. One

Six requested trays divided by six trays per case equals one case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

S18 requests six each, meaning six individual trays. Six trays per case makes that one case equivalent. Six cases would be thirty-six trays: thirty more than requested. The conversion applies only to this stated item and pack size.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pick ticket
Record specifying items and quantities to select for an order.read the pick ticket
unit of measure
Defined basis for expressing a quantity, often UOM.confirm the unit of measure
each
Counting unit meaning one individual item.order six each
case
Grouped package containing the stated number of items.verify the case quantity
case pack
Number of individual items in one standard case.confirm the case pack
pack size
Quantity contained in a particular packaged unit.check the pack size
individual unit
One item counted separately from grouped packaging.count individual units
requested quantity
Amount specified by the order or picking instruction.preserve the requested quantity
case equivalent
Number of cases corresponding to a stated item quantity.calculate the case equivalent
conversion factor
Numeric relationship used to convert one unit to another.verify the conversion factor
per case
For each single case.state six trays per case
total units
Complete number of individual items represented.calculate the total units
overpick
Selection of more units than requested.prevent an overpick
underpick
Selection of fewer units than requested.identify an underpick
full-case pick
Selection of a complete case under the picking process.distinguish a full-case pick
each pick
Selection measured in individual units rather than cases.clarify an each pick
inner pack
Smaller grouped package within a larger case, where used.distinguish an inner pack
outer case
Larger package containing individual items or inner packs.identify the outer case
product-specific conversion
Unit relationship that applies to the particular item.use a product-specific conversion
order unit
Unit in which the order quantity is stated.confirm the order unit
stocking unit
Unit used to record inventory for the relevant item.check the stocking unit
quantity read-back
Repetition of both number and unit to verify accuracy.give a quantity read-back
pick confirmation
Record that the actual required picking work has been completed.distinguish pick confirmation
quantity correction
Amendment of an incorrect interpretation or recorded quantity.explain the quantity correction

Equivalent quantities do not by themselves establish a completed pick or authorize a particular handling method. Follow the actual order and picking process. For another item, check its unit definitions and pack size again rather than reusing six per case.

C Notice the language

Correct the unit before debating the number

Two coworkers can agree that the number is six and still prepare different quantities. State the unit attached to the order, the units per case for this item, and the resulting total. Make the correction about the shared record rather than the other person being careless.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the ticket
Let us check pick ticket S18.
Read number and unit
It requests six each.
Expand each
That means six individual trays.
State the pack size
This item has six trays per standard case.
Calculate the equivalent
Six divided by six gives one case.
Correct the misunderstanding
Six cases would be thirty-six trays.
Quantify the overpick
That would be thirty trays more than requested.
Preserve the order
The requested total remains six trays.
Limit the conversion
This pack size applies to this item.
Avoid a universal assumption
We must check the pack size for other products separately.
Ask for a read-back
Please repeat both the number and the unit.
Distinguish packaging
An outer case is not the same as one individual item.
Confirm the agreed interpretation
One standard case contains the six requested trays.
Separate calculation and completion
We have clarified the quantity, not confirmed the pick.
Avoid changing the order
The ticket has not been increased to six cases.
Close the correction
S18 is six trays in total, equivalent to one case for this item.
6 language and precision notes
Each
Functions as a unit and cannot be silently replaced with cases.
Per
States a ratio: six trays for one case.
Six divided by six
Converts requested individual units into the stated case equivalent.
Six times six
Calculates the mistaken six-case total of thirty-six trays.
This item
Limits the conversion and prevents an unsupported rule for other products.
Equivalent versus completed
Correct arithmetic does not record that any picking work has occurred.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which read-back is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. Six trays total, equivalent to one standard case of this item.

Six each divided by the six-tray case pack gives one case and six trays total.

2. How many trays would six cases contain?

Answer and explanation

C. Thirty-six

Six cases multiplied by six trays per case gives thirty-six trays.

3. How many excess trays would the mistaken six-case pick contain?

Answer and explanation

B. Thirty

Thirty-six trays minus the six requested trays gives thirty excess trays.

4. Which statement wrongly generalizes the pack size?

Answer and explanation

A. Every product in the warehouse has six units per case.

The supplied conversion applies to this tray item, not every warehouse product.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Six each is not six cases

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Warehouse coworker / Asha: Picker

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • overpick
  • case pack
  • total units
  • quantity read-back
  • product-specific conversion
  • requested quantity
  • case equivalent
  • pack size
  • unit of measure
  • Pick confirmation
  1. Omar
    Asha, can you check this before I confirm the pick? S18 says six, and I was about to read that as six cases.
  2. Asha
    Read the 1 beside it. It's six each: six individual trays. The number alone isn't the instruction.
  3. Omar
    You're right; I skipped each. What is the pack for this tray? I need the conversion for this item, not the last one I picked.
  4. Asha
    Its 2 is six trays. One standard case contains the complete six-tray quantity on S18.
  5. Omar
    Then the case count is one. Six trays required, divided by six trays in a case. Have I got that the right way round?
  6. Asha
    Yes, the 3 is one. Say six trays total as well, so nobody hears one and assumes you mean one tray.
  7. Omar
    If I'd taken six cases, I'd have thirty-six trays. I was multiplying the request instead of converting it.
  8. Asha
    Those would be the 4 in the mistaken pick. Thirty-six is thirty above what this ticket asks for.
  9. Omar
    So the excess would be thirty trays, not five. Five is the difference in cases, using this particular case pack.
  10. Asha
    Exactly. An 5 needs its unit stated too. Five extra cases and thirty extra trays describe the same excess here.
  11. Omar
    I shouldn't use six per case for the folders on my next ticket, though. Their cartons look similar.
  12. Asha
    No. This is a 6. The folder pack must come from the folder record, not the appearance of its carton.
  13. Omar
    What about another size of tray? Same product family, different item code. I'd check that separately as well.
  14. Asha
    Yes, confirm the 7 for the exact item. A different variant may have a different quantity per case.
  15. Omar
    Let me try the radio message: S18, six storage trays each, one standard case equivalent. No pick confirmation yet.
  16. Asha
    That 8 is clear. I'd say six individual trays rather than six trays each, which can sound awkward.
  17. Omar
    Six individual trays total. Thanks. We've corrected my reading, but I haven't said the stock has been picked or sent.
  18. Asha
    Right. 9 belongs to the actual work record. The calculation cannot stand in for it.
  19. Omar
    I'll use the corrected quantity in the proper picking process. I won't change the order to six cases just because that was my first reading.
  20. Asha
    Good. The 10 remains six trays. We caught the unit error before calling the pick complete.

Answers and explanations
  1. unit of measure Turn 2. The unit beside six is each, so the requested quantity is six individual trays rather than six cases.
  2. case pack Turn 4. This particular storage-tray item contains six trays per standard case, as stated in the briefing.
  3. case equivalent Turn 6. Six requested trays divided by six trays per case gives one case equivalent, without changing the requested total.
  4. total units Turn 8. Six cases multiplied by six trays per case produces thirty-six individual trays, not the six requested.
  5. overpick Turn 10. An overpick is a quantity above the request; here the proposed six cases would exceed demand by thirty trays.
  6. product-specific conversion Turn 12. The six-tray relationship belongs to this item; it does not establish a conversion for folders or other products.
  7. pack size Turn 14. Pack size is the quantity inside the actual item's package, which can differ between variants and products.
  8. quantity read-back Turn 16. The read-back preserves the six individual trays and one-case equivalent while keeping picking status explicit.
  9. Pick confirmation Turn 18. Pick confirmation records actual completed picking; a correct conversion alone does not establish that completion.
  10. requested quantity Turn 20. S18 still requests six individual trays; the conversation corrects an interpretation rather than amending demand.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: Asha, can you check this before I confirm the pick? S18 says six, and I was about to read that as six cases.
  2. Asha: Read the unit of measure beside it. It's six each: six individual trays. The number alone isn't the instruction.
  3. Omar: You're right; I skipped each. What is the pack for this tray? I need the conversion for this item, not the last one I picked.
  4. Asha: Its case pack is six trays. One standard case contains the complete six-tray quantity on S18.
  5. Omar: Then the case count is one. Six trays required, divided by six trays in a case. Have I got that the right way round?
  6. Asha: Yes, the case equivalent is one. Say six trays total as well, so nobody hears one and assumes you mean one tray.
  7. Omar: If I'd taken six cases, I'd have thirty-six trays. I was multiplying the request instead of converting it.
  8. Asha: Those would be the total units in the mistaken pick. Thirty-six is thirty above what this ticket asks for.
  9. Omar: So the excess would be thirty trays, not five. Five is the difference in cases, using this particular case pack.
  10. Asha: Exactly. An overpick needs its unit stated too. Five extra cases and thirty extra trays describe the same excess here.
  11. Omar: I shouldn't use six per case for the folders on my next ticket, though. Their cartons look similar.
  12. Asha: No. This is a product-specific conversion. The folder pack must come from the folder record, not the appearance of its carton.
  13. Omar: What about another size of tray? Same product family, different item code. I'd check that separately as well.
  14. Asha: Yes, confirm the pack size for the exact item. A different variant may have a different quantity per case.
  15. Omar: Let me try the radio message: S18, six storage trays each, one standard case equivalent. No pick confirmation yet.
  16. Asha: That quantity read-back is clear. I'd say six individual trays rather than six trays each, which can sound awkward.
  17. Omar: Six individual trays total. Thanks. We've corrected my reading, but I haven't said the stock has been picked or sent.
  18. Asha: Right. Pick confirmation belongs to the actual work record. The calculation cannot stand in for it.
  19. Omar: I'll use the corrected quantity in the proper picking process. I won't change the order to six cases just because that was my first reading.
  20. Asha: Good. The requested quantity remains six trays. We caught the unit error before calling the pick complete.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Six trays rather than six cases

Two pickers clarify the counting unit on S18.

  1. Picker: The pick ticket for S18 says six each; I initially read that as six cases.
  2. Partner: The unit of measure is each: six individual trays, not six case packs.
  3. Picker: The product record says six per case. Does that make the requested quantity one complete case?
  4. Partner: Yes, for this item. One case contains the six trays requested on this ticket.
  5. Picker: I'll repeat it: six individual trays, corresponding to one case of this specific product.
  6. Partner: That's right. Please keep the case pack linked to this item rather than every tray.
  7. Picker: I noticed the wider tray beside it has eight per case, which could confuse someone.
  8. Partner: Exactly. The same word tray doesn't establish the same unit count or item identifier.
  9. Picker: I'll confirm the code and unit together when I pass the pick to packing.
  10. Partner: Then packing should hear six trays for S18, not the ambiguous instruction to check six.
A pair is the selling unit

A picker clarifies a glove order with the order desk.

  1. Picker: This ticket requests eight pairs of gloves. The product photo shows two gloves together.
  2. Order clerk: That's because a pair is the selling unit: one left glove and one right glove.
  3. Picker: So eight pairs means sixteen individual gloves, not eight gloves divided into four pairs?
  4. Order clerk: Correct. Keep the order quantity expressed as eight pairs so the unit stays clear.
  5. Picker: The storage label also lists cartons of twelve pairs. That isn't the requested quantity here.
  6. Order clerk: Right. The carton pack describes the supplier packaging, not how many pairs this order needs.
  7. Picker: I'll report eight pairs picked rather than just saying eight, which could mean single gloves.
  8. Order clerk: Please include the size as well; this order is for medium, not large.
  9. Picker: Medium gloves, eight pairs, item GM-2. I'll keep those details together in the handoff.
  10. Order clerk: That matches the order. The photo doesn't replace checking the specified unit and size.
The decimal belongs to weight

A picker checks a stock line with a warehouse clerk.

  1. Picker: My screen shows twelve units and 2.5 beside them. Is the second figure another quantity?
  2. Clerk: That field is listed weight in kilograms per unit, not an extra picking amount.
  3. Picker: The narrow display hid the field heading. I wanted to check before interpreting the decimal.
  4. Clerk: Good question. The order quantity remains twelve units of item WT-8 on this ticket.
  5. Picker: So I should not say twelve and a half, or convert the weight into a unit count?
  6. Clerk: No. Those figures describe different measurements, and the task doesn't ask you to combine them.
  7. Picker: Please include the weight-field heading in the spoken handoff to packing.
  8. Clerk: I'll say twelve units requested; listed weight is two point five kilograms per unit.
  9. Picker: Thank you. I'll read the quantity and the item code together when confirming the pick.
  10. Clerk: That keeps the number twelve attached to units and the decimal attached to its weight field.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Six each is not six cases
  1. Read six individual trays and one standard case equivalent.
  2. Contrast thirty-six trays with six requested; say thirty excess trays, not five trays.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Convert a different pack size

Pick ticket T29 requests twelve each of a folder. This item has four folders per standard case. A coworker misreads the request as twelve cases. No picking work has yet been confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Picker: "The order requests twelve individual ___."

Answer and explanation

B. folders

Each refers to individual folders, not full cases of folders.

2. Coworker: "There are ___ folders per case."

Answer and explanation

A. four

Four is the stated product-specific pack size in this new scenario.

3. Picker: "The correct case equivalent is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. three

Twelve requested folders divided by four folders per case equals three cases.

4. Coworker: "Twelve cases would contain ___ folders."

Answer and explanation

D. forty-eight

Twelve cases multiplied by four folders per case would produce forty-eight folders.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 03: Picking the right unit and quantity
Original fictional case: Pick ticket S18 requests six each of a storage tray. For this item, one standard case contains six trays. Coworker Omar initially reads the request as six cases, which would contain thirty-six trays. Picker Asha clarifies that the requested six individual trays equal one standard case of this item. The correction does not establish the pack size of any other product, authorize a different quantity, or confirm that picking and dispatch have already been completed.
Language workshop: Six each is not six cases
Communication goal: Resolve a unit-of-measure misunderstanding, calculate the product-specific case equivalent, and confirm the requested total.
Language guidance: S18 requests six each, meaning six individual trays. Six trays per case makes that one case equivalent. Six cases would be thirty-six trays: thirty more than requested. The conversion applies only to this stated item and pack size.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Let us check pick ticket S18. / It requests six each. / That means six individual trays. / This item has six trays per standard case. / Six divided by six gives one case. / Six cases would be thirty-six trays. / That would be thirty trays more than requested. / The requested total remains six trays. / This pack size applies to this item. / We must check the pack size for other products separately. / Please repeat both the number and the unit. / An outer case is not the same as one individual item. / One standard case contains the six requested trays. / We have clarified the quantity, not confirmed the pick. / The ticket has not been increased to six cases. / S18 is six trays in total, equivalent to one case for this item.
Vocabulary: pick ticket: Record specifying items and quantities to select for an order. / unit of measure: Defined basis for expressing a quantity, often UOM. / each: Counting unit meaning one individual item. / case: Grouped package containing the stated number of items. / case pack: Number of individual items in one standard case. / pack size: Quantity contained in a particular packaged unit. / individual unit: One item counted separately from grouped packaging. / requested quantity: Amount specified by the order or picking instruction. / case equivalent: Number of cases corresponding to a stated item quantity. / conversion factor: Numeric relationship used to convert one unit to another. / per case: For each single case. / total units: Complete number of individual items represented. / overpick: Selection of more units than requested. / underpick: Selection of fewer units than requested. / full-case pick: Selection of a complete case under the picking process. / each pick: Selection measured in individual units rather than cases. / inner pack: Smaller grouped package within a larger case, where used. / outer case: Larger package containing individual items or inner packs. / product-specific conversion: Unit relationship that applies to the particular item. / order unit: Unit in which the order quantity is stated. / stocking unit: Unit used to record inventory for the relevant item. / quantity read-back: Repetition of both number and unit to verify accuracy. / pick confirmation: Record that the actual required picking work has been completed. / quantity correction: Amendment of an incorrect interpretation or recorded quantity.
Speaking task: Read six individual trays and one standard case equivalent. Contrast thirty-six trays with six requested; say thirty excess trays, not five trays. Read the pick-confirmation exchange without claiming the goods have been picked.
Partner: Omar: Warehouse coworker / Asha: Picker

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Reporting a short pick and replenishment needReserve stock is not a delivery promise+

Your goal

Quantify a pick-face shortfall, distinguish system reserve stock from available stock, and escalate against a stated cutoff.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Reserve stock is not a delivery promise

Order D55 requires ten baskets, but picker Elena finds four at pick face B04. The system lists twelve baskets in reserve. Their allocation, availability, and replenishment timing have not been checked. Replenishment clerk Dev can review the situation. The order cutoff is 15:00, but no promise to complete the order by then is supported. Elena must report the six-basket shortfall without declaring a warehouse-wide stockout or assuming the reserve quantity is already available to D55.

Elena: Picker / Dev: Replenishment clerk

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many baskets does D55 require?

Answer and explanation

D. Ten

Ten is the order requirement against which the pick-face quantity is compared.

2. What is the shortfall at B04?

Answer and explanation

B. Six baskets

Ten needed minus four present at the pick face gives six baskets short.

3. What is known about the twelve in reserve?

Answer and explanation

D. They are listed in the system; allocation and availability are unchecked

The system figure alone does not establish allocation, availability, or replenishment timing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

D55 needs ten baskets and B04 has four, so the location is six short. Twelve shown in reserve is a possible source to investigate, not proof that six can be allocated and replenished in time for 15:00.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pick face
Location from which items are normally selected for orders.check the pick face
reserve stock
Stock recorded in a storage area used to support picking needs.check reserve stock
replenishment
Supplying stock to a picking location from another source.request replenishment
short pick
Picking shortfall against the requested quantity.report a short pick
order requirement
Quantity or specification needed to fulfill an order.state the order requirement
pick-face quantity
Number of units currently present at the picking location.confirm the pick-face quantity
shortfall
Amount needed beyond what is currently present.quantify the shortfall
system quantity
Amount shown in the inventory system.distinguish system quantity
allocation
Assignment of stock to a particular order or demand.check stock allocation
reservation
Earmarking of inventory under the actual system process.verify a stock reservation
available stock
Stock that is actually usable for the relevant demand under the process.confirm available stock
on-hand balance
Recorded quantity held, not automatically free for a particular order.examine the on-hand balance
replenishment request
Request to review or supply stock for a picking location.raise a replenishment request
replenishment task
Assigned work to move stock to the required location.confirm the replenishment task
release status
Whether stock is authorized for the relevant use.check release status
stockout
Absence of available stock at the relevant scope.avoid an unsupported stockout claim
location shortfall
Insufficient quantity at a specified location.report the location shortfall
order cutoff
Latest stated time relevant to processing the order.state the order cutoff
fulfillment commitment
Promise to supply the required order under stated conditions.avoid an unsupported fulfillment commitment
timing estimate
Provisional expectation for when work may be completed.request a timing estimate
demand
Quantity needed by orders or other requirements.review competing demand
FEFO
First-expire, first-out: prioritizing eligible stock by expiry rather than receipt date.apply FEFO to eligible batches
availability check
Verification of stock usable for the specific requirement.complete an availability check
remaining shelf life
Time between the relevant delivery date and expiry, assessed against customer requirements.check remaining shelf life

On-hand, reserved, allocated, released, and available may describe different inventory states. Use the actual system definitions. A short pick at one location does not prove a warehouse-wide stockout, and a replenishment request does not prove movement has occurred.

C Notice the language

Give the shortage and the possible source separately

Saying we have twelve in reserve can sound like a promise that the order is covered. Pair the system quantity with the checks still needed. A useful escalation includes the required quantity, current pick-face quantity, location, order reference, and cutoff.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the demand
D55 needs ten baskets.
Name the location
The pick face is B04.
State the observed quantity
There are four baskets at B04.
Calculate the shortfall
We are six short at the pick face.
State the system figure
The system lists twelve in reserve.
Preserve the uncertainty
Allocation and availability have not been checked.
Avoid promising the reserve
I cannot say those twelve are free for D55.
Ask for review
Can the replenishment desk review the requirement?
Keep the cutoff visible
The order cutoff is 15:00.
Avoid a timing promise
We do not yet have a confirmed replenishment time.
Limit the shortage claim
This is a B04 shortfall, not a confirmed warehouse-wide stockout.
Separate request and movement
The request does not mean the stock has moved.
Confirm the needed amount
Six more baskets would meet the ten-basket requirement.
Preserve the order reference
Keep the review attached to D55.
Avoid automatic priority
The cutoff needs review, not an invented priority approval.
Close with next checks
We need allocation, availability, and timing checked before promising fulfillment.
6 language and precision notes
Six short
Measures the gap between ten required and four present.
Shown in reserve
Reports a system figure without asserting physical or allocation verification.
Free for
Informal wording for stock available to the specific order, not merely present.
At B04
Limits the observed shortage to the named pick face.
By 15:00
Is not promised merely because fifteen hundred is the cutoff.
Request raised
Indicates a next action, not a completed replenishment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which escalation contains the essential facts?

Answer and explanation

D. D55 needs ten; B04 has four; twelve shown in reserve are unchecked; cutoff 15:00.

The complete message retains the demand, observed quantity, uncertain source, and cutoff.

2. How many additional baskets are needed at the pick face?

Answer and explanation

C. Six

Ten required minus four present leaves a six-basket shortfall.

3. Which claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. All twelve reserve baskets are available for D55.

The reserve figure has not been checked for allocation or actual availability to this order.

4. What must happen before a supported completion promise?

Answer and explanation

B. Review stock allocation, availability, and replenishment timing.

Quantity in reserve alone does not establish usable stock or arrival at the pick face in time.

E Conversations

Reserve stock is not a delivery promise

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Picker / Dev: Replenishment clerk

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • replenishment request
  • order cutoff
  • system quantity
  • timing estimate
  • allocation
  • pick face
  • availability check
  • replenishment desk
  • location shortfall
  • fulfillment commitment
  1. Elena
    Dev, D55 is short at B04: ten baskets needed, four here. The cutoff is fifteen hundred.
  2. Dev
    You are six short at the 1. Does the system show any reserve stock for the same item, and has its availability been checked?
  3. Elena
    Twelve shown in reserve, but I haven't checked whether they're allocated. I can't say they're free for us.
  4. Dev
    Keep that distinction. The 2 is a possible source for review, not confirmation that the stock can be assigned and brought to B04.
  5. Elena
    Then I'll report ten needed, four here, six short, twelve in reserve unchecked. What else do you need?
  6. Dev
    Include the 3 of 15:00 as well. That tells us why timing matters without claiming that replenishment can definitely meet it.
  7. Elena
    I'll include D55 and the exact item, so we don't get a different basket sent to B04.
  8. Dev
    Right. A 4 needs the exact demand and location. Raising it does not mean a replenishment task has already been completed.
  9. Elena
    Could those twelve already be committed? I can see the balance, not the allocation detail.
  10. Dev
    That is why 5 needs checking. Twelve on the screen does not necessarily mean twelve uncommitted baskets for D55.
  11. Elena
    So I can't tell packing we'll have ten in time just because twelve appears on the screen.
  12. Dev
    Correct. We need an 6 for this requirement, followed by a realistic timing assessment. The arithmetic alone does not supply those results.
  13. Elena
    I won't call it a warehouse-wide stockout, either. My count is only for B04.
  14. Dev
    Exactly. It is a 7 of six baskets. We should not expand that into a claim that no baskets are available anywhere in the warehouse.
  15. Elena
    Six more at B04 would cover the quantity. We still need to know if those six can get here in time.
  16. Dev
    Yes. Any 8 must follow the actual review. No confirmed replenishment time has been supplied so far.
  17. Elena
    Can you take the review? I'll keep the order and location together with the unanswered stock and timing questions.
  18. Dev
    I can review it at the 9. Keep D55, ten required, four at B04, six short, twelve shown in reserve, and the 15:00 cutoff together.
  19. Elena
    Thanks. I'll tell packing you've taken the review, with no confirmed replenishment time or completion promise.
  20. Dev
    Good. A 10 still needs support. We are taking the next review step, with allocation, availability, and replenishment timing still unresolved.

Answers and explanations
  1. pick face Turn 2. Pick face is B04, where four baskets are present against the order requirement of ten.
  2. system quantity Turn 4. System quantity is the twelve shown in reserve, not verified stock available to D55.
  3. order cutoff Turn 6. Order cutoff is fifteen hundred and must accompany the request without being turned into a completion promise.
  4. replenishment request Turn 8. Replenishment request starts review of supplying the correct stock to B04; it is not a completed movement.
  5. allocation Turn 10. Allocation identifies stock assigned to particular demand and must be checked before assuming the reserve is free.
  6. availability check Turn 12. Availability check establishes usable stock for the specific order rather than relying only on a reserve balance.
  7. location shortfall Turn 14. Location shortfall limits the observed problem to B04 and avoids an unsupported claim about all warehouse stock.
  8. timing estimate Turn 16. Timing estimate remains to be established; neither the reserve quantity nor the cutoff supplies a replenishment completion time.
  9. replenishment desk Turn 18. Replenishment desk is the responsible review point, not a guarantee that stock has been allocated or moved.
  10. fulfillment commitment Turn 20. Fulfillment commitment would promise the order outcome, which remains unsupported until stock and timing checks are complete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: Dev, D55 is short at B04: ten baskets needed, four here. The cutoff is fifteen hundred.
  2. Dev: You are six short at the pick face. Does the system show any reserve stock for the same item, and has its availability been checked?
  3. Elena: Twelve shown in reserve, but I haven't checked whether they're allocated. I can't say they're free for us.
  4. Dev: Keep that distinction. The system quantity is a possible source for review, not confirmation that the stock can be assigned and brought to B04.
  5. Elena: Then I'll report ten needed, four here, six short, twelve in reserve unchecked. What else do you need?
  6. Dev: Include the order cutoff of 15:00 as well. That tells us why timing matters without claiming that replenishment can definitely meet it.
  7. Elena: I'll include D55 and the exact item, so we don't get a different basket sent to B04.
  8. Dev: Right. A replenishment request needs the exact demand and location. Raising it does not mean a replenishment task has already been completed.
  9. Elena: Could those twelve already be committed? I can see the balance, not the allocation detail.
  10. Dev: That is why allocation needs checking. Twelve on the screen does not necessarily mean twelve uncommitted baskets for D55.
  11. Elena: So I can't tell packing we'll have ten in time just because twelve appears on the screen.
  12. Dev: Correct. We need an availability check for this requirement, followed by a realistic timing assessment. The arithmetic alone does not supply those results.
  13. Elena: I won't call it a warehouse-wide stockout, either. My count is only for B04.
  14. Dev: Exactly. It is a location shortfall of six baskets. We should not expand that into a claim that no baskets are available anywhere in the warehouse.
  15. Elena: Six more at B04 would cover the quantity. We still need to know if those six can get here in time.
  16. Dev: Yes. Any timing estimate must follow the actual review. No confirmed replenishment time has been supplied so far.
  17. Elena: Can you take the review? I'll keep the order and location together with the unanswered stock and timing questions.
  18. Dev: I can review it at the replenishment desk. Keep D55, ten required, four at B04, six short, twelve shown in reserve, and the 15:00 cutoff together.
  19. Elena: Thanks. I'll tell packing you've taken the review, with no confirmed replenishment time or completion promise.
  20. Dev: Good. A fulfillment commitment still needs support. We are taking the next review step, with allocation, availability, and replenishment timing still unresolved.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Six baskets short at B04

A picker calls the replenishment desk about D55.

  1. Picker: D55 needs ten baskets, but B04 has four. The short pick is six units.
  2. Replenishment clerk: I see twelve listed in reserve. Have you confirmed whether those are allocated elsewhere?
  3. Picker: No, that's the check I need. The system quantity alone doesn't show availability for D55.
  4. Replenishment clerk: I'll review allocation and the replenishment request. What carrier cutoff affects this order?
  5. Picker: Three o'clock. Please tell me the status before then, even if the six remain unavailable.
  6. Replenishment clerk: Understood. Accepting your request doesn't mean the pick location has already been replenished.
  7. Picker: I'll keep the pick status at four available, six outstanding until the actual stock change is confirmed.
  8. Replenishment clerk: That's accurate. If reserve belongs to another order, I'll report that constraint specifically.
  9. Picker: Please also name the next update time so packing doesn't assume the remaining six are already coming.
  10. Replenishment clerk: I can update you at two fifteen with availability and the replenishment status separately.
The reserve quantity is committed elsewhere

A picker discusses unavailable reserve stock with an allocation clerk.

  1. Picker: The screen shows twenty lamps in reserve. Could those cover my five-unit shortage on L30?
  2. Allocation clerk: They are allocated to order L22, so I can't describe them as free for L30.
  3. Picker: Thanks. I need to explain why visible reserve stock isn't automatically available for my pick.
  4. Allocation clerk: You can say stock exists but is assigned elsewhere. Reallocation would require a separate decision.
  5. Picker: I won't mark the five supplied merely because someone could potentially approve that change.
  6. Allocation clerk: Correct. I can send the request for review, but its outcome is not known.
  7. Picker: Please include L30's five-unit shortage and afternoon cutoff so the review has the actual constraint.
  8. Allocation clerk: I will. Keep the physical reserve count separate from the quantity available to your order.
  9. Picker: Then my update to packing is five outstanding, with reallocation requested but not confirmed.
  10. Allocation clerk: That captures it. I'll return the decision when the responsible planner has reviewed the competing orders.
Replenished in the record, not at the location

A picker checks a task-status discrepancy with a stock associate.

  1. Picker: R28 shows complete, but its named pick location is empty.
  2. Stock associate: Which task and location are you referring to? I need those before comparing the movement record.
  3. Picker: Task R28 for item CF-6, destination B09. I haven't checked any other storage position.
  4. Stock associate: The completion entry names B90. That could explain the discrepancy, but the physical location needs verification.
  5. Picker: Please don't change my pick to B90 solely because those characters appear in the record.
  6. Stock associate: Agreed. I'll ask the movement team to check the recorded destination and report the actual finding.
  7. Picker: I'll tell packing the replenishment status is disputed, rather than simply say the stock disappeared.
  8. Stock associate: That is more accurate. We have a record-location mismatch, not a confirmed loss.
  9. Picker: Could you keep R28 attached to the investigation so the next shift can follow it?
  10. Stock associate: I'll take the location check and update the pick desk with the findings.

Answer and explanation

B. nine

Fourteen required minus five present creates a nine-bin shortfall.

Answer and explanation

B. twenty

Twenty is the displayed reserve balance, not verified available stock.

3. Picker: "Allocation and availability remain ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unchecked

The reserve balance has not been assessed for this specific order.

4. Clerk: "The order cutoff is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 16:30

Sixteen thirty is the stated deadline, not a promised replenishment completion.

Lesson 04: Reporting a short pick and replenishment need
Original fictional case: Order D55 requires ten baskets, but picker Elena finds four at pick face B04. The system lists twelve baskets in reserve. Their allocation, availability, and replenishment timing have not been checked. Replenishment clerk Dev can review the situation. The order cutoff is 15:00, but no promise to complete the order by then is supported. Elena must report the six-basket shortfall without declaring a warehouse-wide stockout or assuming the reserve quantity is already available to D55.
Language workshop: Reserve stock is not a delivery promise
Communication goal: Quantify a pick-face shortfall, distinguish system reserve stock from available stock, and escalate against a stated cutoff.
Language guidance: D55 needs ten baskets and B04 has four, so the location is six short. Twelve shown in reserve is a possible source to investigate, not proof that six can be allocated and replenished in time for 15:00.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): D55 needs ten baskets. / The pick face is B04. / There are four baskets at B04. / We are six short at the pick face. / The system lists twelve in reserve. / Allocation and availability have not been checked. / I cannot say those twelve are free for D55. / Can the replenishment desk review the requirement? / The order cutoff is 15:00. / We do not yet have a confirmed replenishment time. / This is a B04 shortfall, not a confirmed warehouse-wide stockout. / The request does not mean the stock has moved. / Six more baskets would meet the ten-basket requirement. / Keep the review attached to D55. / The cutoff needs review, not an invented priority approval. / We need allocation, availability, and timing checked before promising fulfillment.
Vocabulary: pick face: Location from which items are normally selected for orders. / reserve stock: Stock recorded in a storage area used to support picking needs. / replenishment: Supplying stock to a picking location from another source. / short pick: Picking shortfall against the requested quantity. / order requirement: Quantity or specification needed to fulfill an order. / pick-face quantity: Number of units currently present at the picking location. / shortfall: Amount needed beyond what is currently present. / system quantity: Amount shown in the inventory system. / allocation: Assignment of stock to a particular order or demand. / reservation: Earmarking of inventory under the actual system process. / available stock: Stock that is actually usable for the relevant demand under the process. / on-hand balance: Recorded quantity held, not automatically free for a particular order. / replenishment request: Request to review or supply stock for a picking location. / replenishment task: Assigned work to move stock to the required location. / release status: Whether stock is authorized for the relevant use. / stockout: Absence of available stock at the relevant scope. / location shortfall: Insufficient quantity at a specified location. / order cutoff: Latest stated time relevant to processing the order. / fulfillment commitment: Promise to supply the required order under stated conditions. / timing estimate: Provisional expectation for when work may be completed. / demand: Quantity needed by orders or other requirements. / FEFO: First-expire, first-out: prioritizing eligible stock by expiry rather than receipt date. / availability check: Verification of stock usable for the specific requirement. / remaining shelf life: Time between the relevant delivery date and expiry, assessed against customer requirements.
Speaking task: Read ten needed, four at B04, six short, and twelve shown in reserve. Repeat allocation, availability, and timing as three checks still required. Read the 15:00 cutoff without turning it into a replenishment promise.
Partner: Elena: Picker / Dev: Replenishment clerk
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Order E66 needs fourteen bins. Pick face C05 has five. The system lists twenty in reserve, but allocation and availability are unchecked. The order cutoff is 16:30.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: Can you take the review? I'll keep the order and location together with the unanswered stock and timing questions.
Dev: I can review it at the replenishment desk. Keep D55, ten required, four at B04, six short, twelve shown in reserve, and the 15:00 cutoff together.
Elena: Thanks. I'll tell packing you've taken the review, with no confirmed replenishment time or completion promise.
Dev: Good. A fulfillment commitment still needs support. We are taking the next review step, with allocation, availability, and replenishment timing still unresolved.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Warehouse and Distribution English
Professional audience: Warehouse associates, receivers, pickers, packers, returns staff, and inventory clerks
Scope: All orders, goods, locations, quantities, workers, times, and decisions are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not equipment operation, manual handling, warehouse-system configuration, product-safety inspection, or inventory authorization. Follow actual site procedures, training, safety controls, product requirements, and approval limits. The dialogues do not authorize stock movement, relabeling, release for sale, shipment, or record adjustment. Do not enter restricted areas or operate equipment based on a language exercise.
Lesson 04: Reporting a short pick and replenishment need
Original fictional case: Order D55 requires ten baskets, but picker Elena finds four at pick face B04. The system lists twelve baskets in reserve. Their allocation, availability, and replenishment timing have not been checked. Replenishment clerk Dev can review the situation. The order cutoff is 15:00, but no promise to complete the order by then is supported. Elena must report the six-basket shortfall without declaring a warehouse-wide stockout or assuming the reserve quantity is already available to D55.
Language workshop: Reserve stock is not a delivery promise
Communication goal: Quantify a pick-face shortfall, distinguish system reserve stock from available stock, and escalate against a stated cutoff.
Language guidance: D55 needs ten baskets and B04 has four, so the location is six short. Twelve shown in reserve is a possible source to investigate, not proof that six can be allocated and replenished in time for 15:00.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): D55 needs ten baskets. / The pick face is B04. / There are four baskets at B04. / We are six short at the pick face. / The system lists twelve in reserve. / Allocation and availability have not been checked. / I cannot say those twelve are free for D55. / Can the replenishment desk review the requirement? / The order cutoff is 15:00. / We do not yet have a confirmed replenishment time. / This is a B04 shortfall, not a confirmed warehouse-wide stockout. / The request does not mean the stock has moved. / Six more baskets would meet the ten-basket requirement. / Keep the review attached to D55. / The cutoff needs review, not an invented priority approval. / We need allocation, availability, and timing checked before promising fulfillment.
Vocabulary: pick face: Location from which items are normally selected for orders. / reserve stock: Stock recorded in a storage area used to support picking needs. / replenishment: Supplying stock to a picking location from another source. / short pick: Picking shortfall against the requested quantity. / order requirement: Quantity or specification needed to fulfill an order. / pick-face quantity: Number of units currently present at the picking location. / shortfall: Amount needed beyond what is currently present. / system quantity: Amount shown in the inventory system. / allocation: Assignment of stock to a particular order or demand. / reservation: Earmarking of inventory under the actual system process. / available stock: Stock that is actually usable for the relevant demand under the process. / on-hand balance: Recorded quantity held, not automatically free for a particular order. / replenishment request: Request to review or supply stock for a picking location. / replenishment task: Assigned work to move stock to the required location. / release status: Whether stock is authorized for the relevant use. / stockout: Absence of available stock at the relevant scope. / location shortfall: Insufficient quantity at a specified location. / order cutoff: Latest stated time relevant to processing the order. / fulfillment commitment: Promise to supply the required order under stated conditions. / timing estimate: Provisional expectation for when work may be completed. / demand: Quantity needed by orders or other requirements. / FEFO: First-expire, first-out: prioritizing eligible stock by expiry rather than receipt date. / availability check: Verification of stock usable for the specific requirement. / remaining shelf life: Time between the relevant delivery date and expiry, assessed against customer requirements.
Speaking task: Read ten needed, four at B04, six short, and twelve shown in reserve. Repeat allocation, availability, and timing as three checks still required. Read the 15:00 cutoff without turning it into a replenishment promise.
Partner: Elena: Picker / Dev: Replenishment clerk
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Order E66 needs fourteen bins. Pick face C05 has five. The system lists twenty in reserve, but allocation and availability are unchecked. The order cutoff is 16:30.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: Can you take the review? I'll keep the order and location together with the unanswered stock and timing questions.
Dev: I can review it at the replenishment desk. Keep D55, ten required, four at B04, six short, twelve shown in reserve, and the 15:00 cutoff together.
Elena: Thanks. I'll tell packing you've taken the review, with no confirmed replenishment time or completion promise.
Dev: Good. A fulfillment commitment still needs support. We are taking the next review step, with allocation, availability, and replenishment timing still unresolved.
END REFERENCE
05Packing and label discrepanciesThe loose label belongs to another carton+

Your goal

Interrupt a possible labeling error politely, compare carton and recipient references, and distinguish prevention from a completed error.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The loose label belongs to another carton

At packing station 3, packer Rosa has cartons C70 and C71. The screen for C70 names Harbor Shop. A loose label beside C70 names Hillside School and refers to C71. No label has been applied. Quality colleague Arun asks Rosa to pause the label match while both carton references and recipients are checked. The conversation prevents an unverified label application; it does not establish a completed mislabeling, a shipment departure, or permission to relabel either carton from memory.

Arun: Quality colleague / Rosa: Packer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Who is named on the screen for C70?

Answer and explanation

A. Harbor Shop

The C70 screen names Harbor Shop as its recipient.

2. Which carton does the loose Hillside School label reference?

Answer and explanation

B. C71

The loose label explicitly identifies C71, not the nearby C70.

3. What is the current labeling status?

Answer and explanation

D. No label has been applied

The issue is caught before application, so completed-error wording is inaccurate.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

C70 corresponds to Harbor Shop on the screen. The loose Hillside School label references C71. Being physically beside C70 does not make it the correct label for C70. No label has yet been applied.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
packing station
Work area used to prepare goods and packaging for dispatch.identify the packing station
carton reference
Identifier distinguishing one packed carton from another.verify the carton reference
shipping label
Label carrying shipment-routing and identification information.match the shipping label
loose label
Label not yet attached to a package.check the loose label
recipient
Intended receiving person or organization.confirm the recipient
screen record
Information displayed in the relevant system view.compare the screen record
label match
Verification that a label belongs to the intended carton.pause the label match
label application
Attaching a label to the intended package.verify before label application
mislabeling
Applying incorrect identifying information to an item or carton.prevent mislabeling
reference cross-check
Comparison of identifiers across items and records.complete a reference cross-check
adjacent carton
Nearby carton that may belong to a different shipment.distinguish the adjacent carton
workstation mix-up
Confusion between materials or records at one work area.report a workstation mix-up
shipping address
Destination details on the shipment record.confirm the shipping address
order association
Link between a carton and the relevant order.verify the order association
tracking number
Identifier used to follow a particular shipment.check the tracking number
barcode
Machine-readable representation used to identify records or items.verify the barcode
human-readable text
Printed characters a person can read without decoding equipment.compare human-readable text
label printout
Printed label output before or after verification.inspect the label printout
label reprint
Another print of a label under the actual process.authorize a label reprint
record selection
Choice of the system record currently being viewed or used.check the record selection
unapplied
Not yet attached to the carton.keep the label unapplied
verification pause
Temporary pause to resolve a specific uncertainty.request a verification pause
prevention
Action that stops an error before it occurs.distinguish prevention from correction
shipment departure
Actual movement of a shipment away from the facility.confirm shipment departure

The necessary message identifies a potential mismatch and requests verification of both references. Do not describe it as a completed mislabeled shipment or guess a correction from the recipient names alone. Actual label production and application follow the site process.

C Notice the language

Interrupt the action, not the person

A short, specific pause request works better than a vague warning or accusation. Name the conflicting carton and recipient details immediately. If the label has not been applied, preserve that fact rather than describing a mistake as already completed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Request a precise pause
Please pause before applying that label.
Name the station
This is packing station 3.
Identify the screen
The C70 screen names Harbor Shop.
Identify the loose label
This label names Hillside School and references C71.
State the mismatch
The carton reference and the loose label do not match.
Avoid a proximity assumption
Being beside C70 does not make it the C70 label.
Check both cartons
Let us verify C70 and C71 separately.
Preserve application status
No label has been applied yet.
Avoid an accusation
I am flagging a possible mismatch before application.
Keep recipients separate
Harbor Shop and Hillside School are different recipients.
Ask for the full match
Check the carton reference and recipient together.
Avoid a memory correction
Do not relabel from memory.
Distinguish prevention and repair
This is a verification pause, not a completed relabeling.
Keep departure separate
No shipment departure is established by this check.
Read back the pairs
C70 with Harbor Shop; the loose C71 label with Hillside School.
Close with the required check
Both carton-label associations need verification before application.
6 language and precision notes
Beside versus belongs to
Physical proximity does not establish record association.
Before applying
Places the intervention before an error is completed.
Yet
Preserves the fact that no label application has occurred.
Both
Calls for checking each reference rather than assuming the other carton is correct.
Possible mismatch
Flags the risk without accusing someone of a completed mistake.
Recipient and reference
The name alone is not a substitute for checking the identifier.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which interruption is most precise?

Answer and explanation

A. Please pause: C70 names Harbor Shop, but this loose label names C71 and Hillside School.

The specific pause identifies both conflicting reference-recipient pairs before application.

2. Which status report is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. No label has been applied; both references need checking.

The discrepancy is detected before application and no shipment outcome is established.

3. What does the loose label's location prove?

Answer and explanation

B. Only that it is beside C70, not that it belongs to C70.

Physical proximity alone does not establish which record or carton the label belongs to.

4. Which next step preserves accuracy?

Answer and explanation

B. Verify both carton references and recipient associations.

The supplied next step is a reference cross-check, not an assumed correction or departure.

Arun: Quality colleague / Rosa: Packer

  1. Arun
    Rosa, stop a moment before you use that loose label. C70 says Harbor Shop on the screen; the label beside it says Hillside School.
  2. Rosa
    I haven't attached it. I'll check the 1 first. Does the loose one actually say C70?
  3. Arun
    No, it says C71. We're at station 3 with both cartons here. I think we're looking at two different recipient pairs.
  4. Rosa
    I see C71 on the 2, with Hillside School underneath. C70 on the screen is Harbor Shop.
  5. Arun
    Thanks for pausing. I caught it before anything went on, so we don't have a mislabeled carton to report at this point.
  6. Rosa
    Then it's a 3, not a relabeling job. I'll keep the current status accurate.
  7. Arun
    Can you check both references, not just change the one in your hand? I don't want C71 left with an assumed match.
  8. Rosa
    Yes, the 4 covers C70 and C71. I'll use their records rather than swap labels from memory.
  9. Arun
    The print could be perfectly right for C71 and still be wrong for C70. Being next to this carton doesn't make it its label.
  10. Rosa
    Exactly. The 5 comes from the matching record. We need more than the names looking familiar.
  11. Arun
    Read the two pairs back once, please. I nearly said Hillside for C70 myself because I'd just read the loose label.
  12. Rosa
    C70's 6 names Harbor Shop. The loose C71 label names Hillside School. Neither label has been applied.
  13. Arun
    That's what I saw. We don't know why the loose label was there, and there's no need to guess who put it there.
  14. Rosa
    I'll report the possible 7 with the exact references. A useful note doesn't need an accusation.
  15. Arun
    Right. Please don't write corrected wrong label. That would tell the next person a label was already attached and removed.
  16. Rosa
    I'll describe 8 instead: mismatch spotted before application, both references being checked. That's the stage we're at.
  17. Arun
    Once the records are verified, label handling follows the normal process. I haven't checked either carton for dispatch readiness.
  18. Rosa
    Understood. 9 and dispatch readiness are separate from identifying the two recipient names.
  19. Arun
    I'll leave the handoff as station 3, C70 Harbor Shop on screen, loose C71 Hillside School label, no labels applied.
  20. Rosa
    Agreed. We have no 10 to record here. I'll finish the reference check before reporting any later stage.

Answers and explanations
  1. carton reference Turn 2. The carton reference identifies which carton the label belongs to; proximity to C70 is not sufficient.
  2. loose label Turn 4. The unattached label identifies C71 for Hillside School, separate from the C70 screen record.
  3. verification pause Turn 6. The work pauses before application while the conflicting references are verified, not after a completed labeling error.
  4. reference cross-check Turn 8. Both carton identifiers and their recipient records need comparison; checking one does not validate the other.
  5. order association Turn 10. The association links a carton to its relevant order or recipient record, not to whichever label is nearest.
  6. screen record Turn 12. The screen for C70 names Harbor Shop; that information must remain separate from the loose C71 label.
  7. workstation mix-up Turn 14. The arrangement creates a potential mix-up, without proving who caused it or that anything was shipped incorrectly.
  8. prevention Turn 16. The check prevents a possible error before application; it is not evidence of repairing a completed mislabeling.
  9. Label application Turn 18. Application has not happened yet and must follow the verified match and actual packing process.
  10. shipment departure Turn 20. The label check supplies no evidence of shipment departure, and nothing has been described as dispatched.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two neighboring cartons and one loose label

A checker interrupts a label mismatch at packing station 3.

  1. Checker: Please pause at this packing station. C70 names Harbor Shop, but the shipping label says C71.
  2. Packer: I haven't applied anything yet. The other carton beside it is for Hillside School.
  3. Checker: That is the name on this label. Let's compare identifiers rather than rely on bench position.
  4. Packer: Agreed. My screen is still showing C70 because that's the carton I checked first.
  5. Checker: Does the label for Harbor Shop have its own carton reference available for comparison?
  6. Packer: Yes, it shows C70. I can now distinguish the two labels by reference and recipient.
  7. Checker: Then our discrepancy concerns loose labels beside adjacent orders, not confirmed mislabeling of dispatched goods.
  8. Packer: Correct. Neither carton has left the station, and neither label was applied before your check.
  9. Checker: I'll describe that accurately when we explain why the packing task briefly paused.
  10. Packer: Thank you. We'll keep each carton linked to its own record as packing continues.
A scan acknowledged the wrong identifier

A packer clarifies a scan result with a systems support clerk.

  1. Packer: The scanner beeped, but the screen displays a carton reference different from the one I'm checking.
  2. Support clerk: A beep alone doesn't tell us which identifier was registered. What does the confirmation actually show?
  3. Packer: It shows C18, while the carton I'm working on is C81. The digits are reversed.
  4. Support clerk: Then report a reference mismatch, not a successful confirmation of C81. Has packing been completed?
  5. Packer: No. I paused the confirmation because I couldn't reconcile the screen with this carton identifier.
  6. Support clerk: That gives us a clear issue to investigate. I'll check which record received the scan acknowledgment.
  7. Packer: Please don't mark C81 complete simply to match the carton beside me.
  8. Support clerk: I won't. We need to establish the recorded event before deciding on a correction.
  9. Packer: I'll keep the two references visible in the query and describe the observed screen result.
  10. Support clerk: I'll check C18 against C81; this isn't a confirmed general scanner outage.
The old address on a replacement label

A packer queries a replacement label with the shipping desk.

  1. Packer: The replacement label for order H12 has the old address, while the order screen shows the new one.
  2. Shipping clerk: Did the customer change the address after the original label was created for that order?
  3. Packer: The history shows this morning's address update, but the label still differs.
  4. Shipping clerk: Let's compare the label version with the shipping record. A replacement isn't necessarily a fresh-address confirmation.
  5. Packer: I don't want replacement to hide the fact that the destination remains wrong.
  6. Shipping clerk: I can see this copy came from the earlier label record, before the address update.
  7. Packer: So this repeats an old destination; the customer hasn't changed it again?
  8. Shipping clerk: Correct. I'll take the label review and confirm the current destination record before another label is supplied.
  9. Packer: I'll keep H12 pending at this stage rather than describe it as ready for dispatch.
  10. Shipping clerk: Please do. I'll tell you which verified label reference applies when the review is complete.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 05: Packing and label discrepancies
Original fictional case: At packing station 3, packer Rosa has cartons C70 and C71. The screen for C70 names Harbor Shop. A loose label beside C70 names Hillside School and refers to C71. No label has been applied. Quality colleague Arun asks Rosa to pause the label match while both carton references and recipients are checked. The conversation prevents an unverified label application; it does not establish a completed mislabeling, a shipment departure, or permission to relabel either carton from memory.
Language workshop: The loose label belongs to another carton
Communication goal: Interrupt a possible labeling error politely, compare carton and recipient references, and distinguish prevention from a completed error.
Language guidance: C70 corresponds to Harbor Shop on the screen. The loose Hillside School label references C71. Being physically beside C70 does not make it the correct label for C70. No label has yet been applied.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Please pause before applying that label. / This is packing station 3. / The C70 screen names Harbor Shop. / This label names Hillside School and references C71. / The carton reference and the loose label do not match. / Being beside C70 does not make it the C70 label. / Let us verify C70 and C71 separately. / No label has been applied yet. / I am flagging a possible mismatch before application. / Harbor Shop and Hillside School are different recipients. / Check the carton reference and recipient together. / Do not relabel from memory. / This is a verification pause, not a completed relabeling. / No shipment departure is established by this check. / C70 with Harbor Shop; the loose C71 label with Hillside School. / Both carton-label associations need verification before application.
Vocabulary: packing station: Work area used to prepare goods and packaging for dispatch. / carton reference: Identifier distinguishing one packed carton from another. / shipping label: Label carrying shipment-routing and identification information. / loose label: Label not yet attached to a package. / recipient: Intended receiving person or organization. / screen record: Information displayed in the relevant system view. / label match: Verification that a label belongs to the intended carton. / label application: Attaching a label to the intended package. / mislabeling: Applying incorrect identifying information to an item or carton. / reference cross-check: Comparison of identifiers across items and records. / adjacent carton: Nearby carton that may belong to a different shipment. / workstation mix-up: Confusion between materials or records at one work area. / shipping address: Destination details on the shipment record. / order association: Link between a carton and the relevant order. / tracking number: Identifier used to follow a particular shipment. / barcode: Machine-readable representation used to identify records or items. / human-readable text: Printed characters a person can read without decoding equipment. / label printout: Printed label output before or after verification. / label reprint: Another print of a label under the actual process. / record selection: Choice of the system record currently being viewed or used. / unapplied: Not yet attached to the carton. / verification pause: Temporary pause to resolve a specific uncertainty. / prevention: Action that stops an error before it occurs. / shipment departure: Actual movement of a shipment away from the facility.
Speaking task: Read C70 with Harbor Shop and C71 with Hillside School. Repeat the interruption and the statement that no label has been applied. Read the cross-check request for both references, without swapping labels from memory.
Partner: Arun: Quality colleague / Rosa: Packer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At station 5, the screen for carton D80 names Maple Clinic. A loose label beside it names Meadow Cafe and references D81. No label has been applied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Arun: Once the records are verified, label handling follows the normal process. I haven't checked either carton for dispatch readiness.
Rosa: Understood. Label application and dispatch readiness are separate from identifying the two recipient names.
Arun: I'll leave the handoff as station 3, C70 Harbor Shop on screen, loose C71 Hillside School label, no labels applied.
Rosa: Agreed. We have no shipment departure to record here. I'll finish the reference check before reporting any later stage.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
