06Staging and dispatch statusThree staged, two on the manifest+

Your goal

Compare staged goods with a dispatch record and report a cutoff risk without claiming departure or an approved correction.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Three staged, two on the manifest

Order L66 has three cartons staged in lane 2. Its dispatch manifest lists only two cartons. The carrier cutoff is 16:00, and the goods have not departed. Staging colleague Imani asks dispatch clerk Noah to investigate the missing carton entry. No cause or specific missing carton identifier has been established. The discrepancy concerns the recorded carton count, not a verified physically missing carton. A review request does not confirm a corrected manifest, carrier acceptance, or departure before cutoff.

Imani: Staging colleague / Noah: Dispatch clerk

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. How many L66 cartons are staged in lane 2?

Answer and explanation

C. Three

Three physical cartons are staged for L66 in the specified lane.

2. What does the manifest list?

Answer and explanation

C. Two cartons

The dispatch manifest records two cartons, one fewer than the staged count.

3. What is the actual departure status?

Answer and explanation

D. The goods have not departed

The briefing explicitly places all goods before departure, with the record issue unresolved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Three cartons are staged, but only two are listed. The manifest is one carton below the staged count. That is not evidence that a physical carton is missing, and the specific omitted carton reference has not been identified.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
staging
Placing goods in a designated area ahead of a later process.confirm staging status
staging lane
Designated area for grouped outgoing goods.identify the staging lane
dispatch manifest
Record listing shipments or units included in a dispatch.compare the dispatch manifest
staged count
Number physically present in the staging area.verify the staged count
manifest count
Number recorded on the dispatch manifest.state the manifest count
carton entry
Individual or counted carton record in the relevant document.investigate a missing carton entry
record discrepancy
Difference between documented and observed information.report a record discrepancy
carrier cutoff
Latest stated time for the relevant carrier process.confirm the carrier cutoff
departure status
Whether goods have actually left the facility.preserve departure status
carrier acceptance
Carrier's acceptance of goods under the actual process.verify carrier acceptance
dispatch release
Authorization to proceed with the relevant outgoing shipment process.await dispatch release
manifest correction
Authorized amendment to an inaccurate dispatch record.confirm a manifest correction
shipment grouping
Association of cartons or items under one outgoing shipment.verify shipment grouping
lane assignment
Designation of the staging lane for an order.check the lane assignment
outbound order
Order being prepared for shipment from the facility.identify the outbound order
loading status
Whether goods have been loaded, distinct from staging.check loading status
departure confirmation
Evidence or record confirming goods have left.obtain departure confirmation
missing entry
Record absent from the document, not necessarily a missing physical item.investigate the missing entry
dispatch clerk
Worker coordinating outgoing records and shipment information.contact the dispatch clerk
cutoff risk
Possibility that an unresolved issue affects the stated deadline.flag the cutoff risk
reconciliation check
Comparison intended to explain and resolve inconsistent figures.perform a reconciliation check
unverified cause
Explanation not established by the available evidence.avoid an unverified cause
status distinction
Separation of stages that must not be treated as interchangeable.preserve the status distinction
pending correction
Record amendment that has not yet been completed.report a pending correction

Staged, loaded, accepted by a carrier, and departed are separate statuses. The 16:00 cutoff gives urgency but does not prove completion before that time. Report the discrepancy for investigation without inventing an amendment or recording departure prematurely.

C Notice the language

Ready-looking goods may still have unfinished records

Cartons in a staging lane can look ready to leave, but their physical position does not prove the dispatch record is complete. Name the lane, order, physical count, manifest count, and cutoff. Keep the record discrepancy separate from the actual movement status.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the order and lane
L66 is staged in lane 2.
State the physical count
There are three cartons in the lane.
State the document count
The manifest lists two cartons.
Give the discrepancy
The manifest is one carton below the staged count.
Distinguish record and goods
We are investigating a missing entry, not a confirmed missing carton.
Keep the deadline visible
The carrier cutoff is 16:00.
Ask for investigation
Can dispatch check the missing carton entry?
Keep departure accurate
The goods have not departed.
Avoid a completion claim
The manifest has not been corrected yet.
Avoid an invented identifier
We have not identified which carton reference is omitted.
Keep the cause open
We do not know why the record differs.
Separate staging and loading
Staged does not mean loaded.
Separate loading and departure
Loaded would not by itself prove departure.
Avoid a cutoff guarantee
I cannot promise departure before 16:00 from these facts alone.
Read back both counts
Three staged, two listed, with departure still pending.
Close with the next owner
Dispatch will investigate while the current status stays explicit.
6 language and precision notes
One below
Compares the manifest's two against the physical count of three.
Missing entry versus missing carton
Absence in a record is not the same as physical absence.
Staged
Identifies a preparation stage, not a completed carrier handover.
Has not departed
Preserves the actual movement status explicitly.
Cutoff risk
Signals urgency without making an unsupported delivery commitment.
Which carton
Remains a question when only the count mismatch is known.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which status update is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. L66 has three staged in lane 2, two listed, and no departure yet.

The accurate update separates the physical count, document count, and unfinished departure.

2. What is the known discrepancy?

Answer and explanation

C. One carton fewer on the manifest than in staging.

Two listed is one below the three physically staged cartons.

3. Which statement invents an outcome?

Answer and explanation

D. The goods will definitely depart before 16:00.

The deadline and review request do not establish a guaranteed departure outcome.

4. What should Noah investigate?

Answer and explanation

D. The missing carton entry and correct association with L66.

The supplied issue concerns a count mismatch in the dispatch record for L66.

E Conversations

Three staged, two on the manifest

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Staging colleague / Noah: Dispatch clerk

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • manifest correction
  • status distinction
  • dispatch manifest
  • Staging
  • missing entry
  • staged count
  • unverified cause
  • departure confirmation
  • reconciliation check
  • cutoff risk
  1. Imani
    Noah, there's a manifest discrepancy on L66. Three cartons in lane 2, but only two listed. Carrier cutoff is sixteen hundred.
  2. Noah
    I will check the 1. First, are we talking about three cartons physically present, rather than a system quantity you have not verified?
  3. Imani
    Three physically staged, yes. Nothing has departed. I'm comparing what's in lane 2 with the manifest.
  4. Noah
    Then the 2 is three and the document count is two. We should not describe that as a physically missing carton.
  5. Imani
    Please call it a missing entry, not a lost carton. The third carton is here with the other two.
  6. Noah
    That is the right 3. A record can be incomplete while all the staged cartons are present. We still need to establish which entry is missing.
  7. Imani
    I haven't identified which carton reference is omitted yet. I have L66, lane 2, and the count difference.
  8. Noah
    I will investigate the 4 without inventing an identifier. The check needs to connect the correct carton records with L66.
  9. Imani
    Can you flag sixteen hundred with it? Dispatch needs the deadline as well as the discrepancy.
  10. Noah
    I will flag the 5 at 16:00. That gives the issue its timing context, but it does not mean I can promise a completed correction or departure by then.
  11. Imani
    The order may look ready because it's staged. I don't want that turned into a departed status.
  12. Noah
    Yes. 6 is not the same as loading or carrier acceptance, and neither of those should be assumed from their position in lane 2.
  13. Imani
    Have you corrected the record, or are you taking the check now? I need to pass on the right stage.
  14. Noah
    I am taking the investigation. A 7 is not complete, and I should not tell you that the count has been fixed before the check.
  15. Imani
    I haven't established what caused the missing entry. It could be several things; I don't have evidence yet.
  16. Noah
    Keep the 8 out of the factual conclusion. We can report the discrepancy accurately without assigning blame.
  17. Imani
    Read-back: L66, lane 2, three staged, two listed, cutoff sixteen hundred, nothing departed.
  18. Noah
    That is a clear 9 to take forward. I will investigate the omitted entry while keeping those figures and the cutoff in view.
  19. Imani
    I'll name you for the investigation and keep it open. I won't tell the next shift the order has gone.
  20. Noah
    Correct. No 10 exists here. Three staged, two listed, and the manifest question is still open while I investigate.

Answers and explanations
  1. dispatch manifest Turn 2. Dispatch manifest lists two cartons and must be reconciled with the three physically staged for L66.
  2. staged count Turn 4. Staged count is the three cartons physically present, distinct from the manifest's recorded two.
  3. status distinction Turn 6. Status distinction separates the record mismatch from physical stock absence and from actual departure.
  4. missing entry Turn 8. Missing entry is the document issue; its specific carton identifier has not yet been established.
  5. cutoff risk Turn 10. Cutoff risk marks possible impact on the sixteen-hundred deadline without guaranteeing departure or acceptance.
  6. Staging Turn 12. Staging places goods in a preparation area; it does not confirm loading, carrier acceptance, or departure.
  7. manifest correction Turn 14. Manifest correction has not occurred merely because Noah accepts the investigation; the document still requires checking.
  8. unverified cause Turn 16. Unverified cause remains unknown; the observed count difference does not identify an individual or system failure.
  9. reconciliation check Turn 18. Reconciliation check compares the actual staged quantity with the manifest and seeks the correct missing association.
  10. departure confirmation Turn 20. Departure confirmation is absent because the goods have not left; an accepted review task does not change that fact.
Read the complete conversation

  2. Noah: I will check the dispatch manifest. First, are we talking about three cartons physically present, rather than a system quantity you have not verified?

  7. Imani: I haven't identified which carton reference is omitted yet. I have L66, lane 2, and the count difference.
  8. Noah: I will investigate the missing entry without inventing an identifier. The check needs to connect the correct carton records with L66.
  9. Imani: Can you flag sixteen hundred with it? Dispatch needs the deadline as well as the discrepancy.
  10. Noah: I will flag the cutoff risk at 16:00. That gives the issue its timing context, but it does not mean I can promise a completed correction or departure by then.
  11. Imani: The order may look ready because it's staged. I don't want that turned into a departed status.

  13. Imani: Have you corrected the record, or are you taking the check now? I need to pass on the right stage.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Three staged, two on the manifest

An outbound associate updates the dispatch clerk about L66.

  1. Associate: L66 has three cartons staged in lane two, but the load manifest lists only two.
  2. Dispatch clerk: Has anything departed yet, or is the full physical shipment still in the staging area?
  3. Associate: All three remain staged. I haven't described the order as dispatched because the record doesn't match.
  4. Dispatch clerk: Thanks. Which carton identifier is missing from the manifest so I can investigate the specific entry?
  5. Associate: L66-3 is missing; the first and second carton identifiers are both listed correctly.
  6. Dispatch clerk: I'll check that entry before the four p.m. cutoff. Please keep the physical count distinct.
  7. Associate: Will your acknowledgment mean the manifest is corrected, or only that you're checking it?
  8. Dispatch clerk: Only that I'm checking it. I'll confirm the revised status when the comparison is complete.
  9. Associate: Then I'll tell packing three staged, manifest discrepancy pending, no departure confirmation yet.
  10. Dispatch clerk: That gives the next person the actual stage without suggesting the order has already shipped.
The carrier arrived before collection completed

A warehouse associate clarifies a status with customer service.

  1. Customer service clerk: The carrier is here. Can I tell the customer that their order has left the warehouse?
  2. Associate: Not yet. Carrier arrival and this order's dispatch are different events; its cartons are still staged.
  3. Customer service clerk: Do you know whether their shipment is on the load manifest for this particular collection?
  4. Associate: Yes, order W23 is listed, but we don't yet have departure confirmation for it.
  5. Customer service clerk: Then I can say it's scheduled for this collection, rather than already on its way?
  6. Associate: That's supported by the manifest. Please keep the physical departure status pending until dispatch confirms it.
  7. Customer service clerk: The customer asked for a tracking update, and I don't want to repeat a premature status.
  8. Associate: Use the order reference when you ask dispatch; other orders may be at different stages.
  9. Customer service clerk: A carrier's arrival doesn't mean every scheduled order has departed.
  10. Associate: Exactly. W23 is staged and listed; its dispatch confirmation remains pending.
A lane number mistaken for a departure time

An associate checks an ambiguous outbound instruction.

  1. Associate: You said twelve for order J18. Did you mean lane twelve or departure at twelve o'clock?
  2. Dispatch clerk: I meant staging lane twelve. The planned carrier collection is at two this afternoon.
  3. Associate: Thanks. I was about to tell packing we had a noon cutoff, which would be wrong.
  4. Dispatch clerk: The cutoff for this collection is one thirty. Keep the lane, cutoff, and departure estimate separate.
  5. Associate: I'll read it back: lane twelve, cutoff one thirty, planned collection two o'clock.
  6. Dispatch clerk: That's correct. The collection time is planned; it isn't a confirmation that goods have departed.
  7. Associate: Does J18 already appear on the manifest for that carrier, or is that still pending?
  8. Dispatch clerk: It's on the current manifest. Its physical staging status needs confirmation from the outbound team.
  9. Associate: I'll check staging rather than assume the manifest proves the order is ready.
  10. Dispatch clerk: Good. The next update should say whether J18 is actually staged in the assigned lane.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Warehouse and Distribution English
Professional audience: Warehouse associates, receivers, pickers, packers, returns staff, and inventory clerks
Scope: All orders, goods, locations, quantities, workers, times, and decisions are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not equipment operation, manual handling, warehouse-system configuration, product-safety inspection, or inventory authorization. Follow actual site procedures, training, safety controls, product requirements, and approval limits. The dialogues do not authorize stock movement, relabeling, release for sale, shipment, or record adjustment. Do not enter restricted areas or operate equipment based on a language exercise.
Lesson 06: Staging and dispatch status
Original fictional case: Order L66 has three cartons staged in lane 2. Its dispatch manifest lists only two cartons. The carrier cutoff is 16:00, and the goods have not departed. Staging colleague Imani asks dispatch clerk Noah to investigate the missing carton entry. No cause or specific missing carton identifier has been established. The discrepancy concerns the recorded carton count, not a verified physically missing carton. A review request does not confirm a corrected manifest, carrier acceptance, or departure before cutoff.
Language workshop: Three staged, two on the manifest
Communication goal: Compare staged goods with a dispatch record and report a cutoff risk without claiming departure or an approved correction.
Language guidance: Three cartons are staged, but only two are listed. The manifest is one carton below the staged count. That is not evidence that a physical carton is missing, and the specific omitted carton reference has not been identified.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): L66 is staged in lane 2. / There are three cartons in the lane. / The manifest lists two cartons. / The manifest is one carton below the staged count. / We are investigating a missing entry, not a confirmed missing carton. / The carrier cutoff is 16:00. / Can dispatch check the missing carton entry? / The goods have not departed. / The manifest has not been corrected yet. / We have not identified which carton reference is omitted. / We do not know why the record differs. / Staged does not mean loaded. / Loaded would not by itself prove departure. / I cannot promise departure before 16:00 from these facts alone. / Three staged, two listed, with departure still pending. / Dispatch will investigate while the current status stays explicit.
Vocabulary: staging: Placing goods in a designated area ahead of a later process. / staging lane: Designated area for grouped outgoing goods. / dispatch manifest: Record listing shipments or units included in a dispatch. / staged count: Number physically present in the staging area. / manifest count: Number recorded on the dispatch manifest. / carton entry: Individual or counted carton record in the relevant document. / record discrepancy: Difference between documented and observed information. / carrier cutoff: Latest stated time for the relevant carrier process. / departure status: Whether goods have actually left the facility. / carrier acceptance: Carrier's acceptance of goods under the actual process. / dispatch release: Authorization to proceed with the relevant outgoing shipment process. / manifest correction: Authorized amendment to an inaccurate dispatch record. / shipment grouping: Association of cartons or items under one outgoing shipment. / lane assignment: Designation of the staging lane for an order. / outbound order: Order being prepared for shipment from the facility. / loading status: Whether goods have been loaded, distinct from staging. / departure confirmation: Evidence or record confirming goods have left. / missing entry: Record absent from the document, not necessarily a missing physical item. / dispatch clerk: Worker coordinating outgoing records and shipment information. / cutoff risk: Possibility that an unresolved issue affects the stated deadline. / reconciliation check: Comparison intended to explain and resolve inconsistent figures. / unverified cause: Explanation not established by the available evidence. / status distinction: Separation of stages that must not be treated as interchangeable. / pending correction: Record amendment that has not yet been completed.
Speaking task: Read three physically staged and two on the dispatch manifest. Repeat lane 2, L66, and the 16:00 cutoff in one handoff. Contrast a missing entry with a missing physical carton; then read no departure.
Partner: Imani: Staging colleague / Noah: Dispatch clerk
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Order M77 has five cartons staged in lane 4. The manifest lists four. The carrier cutoff is 17:00, and nothing has departed. Dispatch will investigate the missing entry.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Imani: Read-back: L66, lane 2, three staged, two listed, cutoff sixteen hundred, nothing departed.
Noah: That is a clear reconciliation check to take forward. I will investigate the omitted entry while keeping those figures and the cutoff in view.
Imani: I'll name you for the investigation and keep it open. I won't tell the next shift the order has gone.
Noah: Correct. No departure confirmation exists here. Three staged, two listed, and the manifest question is still open while I investigate.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Returns and stock statusUnopened-looking is not ready for sale+

Your goal

Confirm return identity, qualify an external observation, and refer condition review without releasing the item as saleable.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Unopened-looking is not ready for sale

Returns intake worker Theo receives kettle K25, which matches its return reference. The packaging appears unopened, but the required condition review is still pending. Returns checker Mira owns that review. Theo cannot mark the kettle ready for sale. Colleague Hana needs a clear handoff separating reference matching, apparent packaging condition, review status, and release authority. No refund, functional check, product-safety finding, or saleable-stock decision has been completed in this scenario.

Theo: Returns intake worker / Hana: Inventory colleague

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has been matched?

Answer and explanation

D. Kettle K25 and its return reference

The intake check connects the kettle to the correct return reference.

2. What does the packaging observation establish?

Answer and explanation

B. It appears unopened, without completing condition review

Appearance is the stated observation and does not establish the item's internal condition.

3. Who owns the pending review?

Answer and explanation

C. Mira

Mira is explicitly named as responsible for the condition review.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

K25 matches the return reference, and its packaging appears unopened. Neither fact completes the condition review or proves unused, functional, safe, or saleable condition. Mira owns the review, and Theo cannot mark the kettle ready for sale.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
return intake
Initial receiving and recording of returned goods.complete return intake
return reference
Identifier linking goods to a specific return arrangement.match the return reference
returned item
Product brought back through a return process.identify the returned item
reference match
Correspondence between the item and the return record.confirm the reference match
packaging appearance
Visible external state of the packaging.describe packaging appearance
appears unopened
Looks unopened without proving past use or internal condition.report appears unopened
condition review
Examination required to assess returned-item condition.await condition review
returns checker
Person responsible for the relevant return examination.name the returns checker
pending review
Status indicating the required examination is unfinished.preserve pending-review status
saleable stock
Inventory approved as suitable for sale under the applicable process.distinguish saleable stock
ready for sale
Status indicating required sale-release conditions have been met.verify ready-for-sale status
release authority
Permission to approve an item for a particular use or status.confirm release authority
functional check
Examination of whether a product operates as required.distinguish a functional check
product-safety finding
Established conclusion about relevant safety requirements.avoid an unsupported product-safety finding
unused condition
State of not having been used, requiring appropriate evidence.verify unused condition
intake observation
Fact or qualified impression recorded during initial receipt.preserve the intake observation
review owner
Person responsible for completing the pending assessment.identify the review owner
stock status
System or process classification of inventory condition and availability.confirm stock status
restocking
Returning goods to the relevant inventory under the actual process.clarify restocking authorization
disposition
Authorized decision about the next treatment of returned goods.await the disposition
refund status
Whether a financial return to the customer has been approved or completed.keep refund status separate
condition evidence
Information supporting a conclusion about item condition.check condition evidence
review completion
Actual finishing of the required assessment.confirm review completion
status release
Authorized change from a restricted or pending state.distinguish status release

Return identity, physical condition, financial remedy, and inventory status answer different questions. Preserve the actual site statuses and approval limits. This exercise does not instruct learners to test electrical goods, inspect product safety, or release a returned item.

C Notice the language

A careful observation can still be useful

Appears unopened reports what the packaging looks like without claiming that the item has never been used or is safe to sell. Confirm the return reference, name the reviewer, and preserve the pending status instead of letting a positive appearance substitute for the required decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the return
This is kettle K25.
Confirm the reference
It matches the return reference.
Qualify the observation
The packaging appears unopened.
Avoid a use-history claim
I cannot confirm that the kettle has never been used.
State the review status
Condition review is still pending.
Name the owner
Mira owns that review.
Explain your limit
I cannot mark it ready for sale.
Separate identity and condition
A reference match is not a condition approval.
Avoid a functional claim
No functional check is confirmed here.
Avoid a safety claim
We have no established product-safety finding from this intake.
Keep finance separate
This handoff does not confirm a refund.
Preserve the existing status
Keep the review status pending until the required decision.
Ask for an accurate handoff
Please include the observation and the named reviewer.
Avoid a false release
The item has not been approved as saleable stock.
Distinguish assignment and completion
Mira owning the review does not mean it is finished.
Close with the next step
The next step is Mira's condition review.
6 language and precision notes
Appears
Qualifies an observation instead of asserting a fully verified condition.
Unopened versus unused
Packaging appearance does not establish the complete use history of the item.
Matches
Answers identity, not functional condition or saleability.
Pending
Indicates the review is unfinished, not automatically failed or passed.
Owns versus completed
Responsibility for a review is not evidence of its outcome.
Ready for sale
Requires the actual applicable checks and authorization, not a visual impression alone.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. K25 matches its reference; packaging appears unopened; Mira's review is pending.

The statement separates verified identity, qualified observation, and the unfinished review.

2. Which conclusion overstates the observation?

Answer and explanation

D. The kettle is definitely functional and unused.

External packaging appearance does not establish function or prior use.

3. Who has the next defined task?

Answer and explanation

A. Mira, to perform the condition review.

Mira is the named owner of the required pending assessment.

4. Which status should not be claimed yet?

Answer and explanation

C. Ready for sale.

Sale-ready status is unsupported because condition review and release approval remain incomplete.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Unopened-looking is not ready for sale

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Theo: Returns intake worker / Hana: Inventory colleague

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • reference match
  • intake observation
  • stock status
  • release authority
  • Refund status
  • review owner
  • Review completion
  • functional check
  • Condition review
  • disposition
  1. Hana
    Theo, is returned kettle K25 cleared for sale, or is the condition review still outstanding?
  2. Theo
    It has a 1 with the return record. The packaging appears unopened, but the condition review is still pending.
  3. Hana
    The box looks untouched. Are you saying it appears unopened, or that you've confirmed the kettle was never used?
  4. Theo
    It is an 2, not a confirmed history. I cannot say the kettle has never been used based only on how the packaging looks.
  5. Hana
    Then I won't call it ready for sale just because the box looks good. What has actually been completed?
  6. Theo
    Exactly. 3 has not been completed. We have matched the identity, but that does not tell us the result of the required examination.
  7. Hana
    Who's taking the review? I need a named person for the handover, not just someone to look at it.
  8. Theo
    Mira is the 4. She owns the condition review. Assigning that work to her does not mean we already have a decision.
  9. Hana
    Can you release it while we're waiting, or is that outside your authority?
  10. Theo
    No. I do not have that 5. I cannot turn an intake observation into permission to return the kettle to saleable stock.
  11. Hana
    And we haven't established that it works. Matching the return reference isn't a function test.
  12. Theo
    Correct. No 6 is confirmed here. Nor does our conversation establish a product-safety finding or an approved sale-ready condition.
  13. Hana
    What about the refund? I shouldn't tell customer service it's approved from this warehouse note, should I?
  14. Theo
    Yes. 7 is not established by this handoff. Identity, condition, inventory status, and a customer remedy are different questions.
  15. Hana
    I'll read the note back: K25 matched, packaging appears unopened, condition review with Mira, no sale release.
  16. Theo
    That keeps the 8 accurate. It also avoids claiming that the item is either approved or rejected before the review provides its result.
  17. Hana
    So pending isn't the same as rejected, either. We don't have the condition decision yet.
  18. Theo
    Exactly. A 9 still needs to follow the actual review process. We should not choose sale, rejection, repair, or another outcome from appearance alone.
  19. Hana
    I'll pass on exactly that, with Mira as owner. The next shift will know what remains outstanding.
  20. Theo
    Thank you. 10 is still pending with Mira. K25 is matched to the return, but it has not been marked ready for sale.

Answers and explanations
  1. reference match Turn 2. Reference match confirms K25 belongs to the return record; it does not establish condition or saleability.
  2. intake observation Turn 4. Intake observation reports the packaging appearance and does not verify the item's complete use history.
  3. Condition review Turn 6. Condition review is the required next assessment, still unfinished despite the matching reference and apparent packaging state.
  4. review owner Turn 8. Review owner identifies Mira as responsible for the assessment without implying she has already carried it out.
  5. release authority Turn 10. Release authority to mark K25 ready for sale is not held by Theo and is not created by packaging appearance.
  6. functional check Turn 12. Functional check would examine operation; no such completed check is supplied by this intake conversation.
  7. Refund status Turn 14. Refund status concerns a separate financial process and is not established by matching the returned item.
  8. stock status Turn 16. Stock status must preserve the pending review and absence of sale release rather than imply immediate availability.
  9. disposition Turn 18. Disposition is the authorized decision about the returned item, still to follow the required condition review.
  10. Review completion Turn 20. Review completion remains absent; the handoff names the owner but does not supply the assessment or its outcome.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: Theo, is returned kettle K25 cleared for sale, or is the condition review still outstanding?
  2. Theo: It has a reference match with the return record. The packaging appears unopened, but the condition review is still pending.
  3. Hana: The box looks untouched. Are you saying it appears unopened, or that you've confirmed the kettle was never used?
  4. Theo: It is an intake observation, not a confirmed history. I cannot say the kettle has never been used based only on how the packaging looks.
  5. Hana: Then I won't call it ready for sale just because the box looks good. What has actually been completed?
  6. Theo: Exactly. Condition review has not been completed. We have matched the identity, but that does not tell us the result of the required examination.
  7. Hana: Who's taking the review? I need a named person for the handover, not just someone to look at it.
  8. Theo: Mira is the review owner. She owns the condition review. Assigning that work to her does not mean we already have a decision.
  9. Hana: Can you release it while we're waiting, or is that outside your authority?
  10. Theo: No. I do not have that release authority. I cannot turn an intake observation into permission to return the kettle to saleable stock.
  11. Hana: And we haven't established that it works. Matching the return reference isn't a function test.
  12. Theo: Correct. No functional check is confirmed here. Nor does our conversation establish a product-safety finding or an approved sale-ready condition.
  13. Hana: What about the refund? I shouldn't tell customer service it's approved from this warehouse note, should I?
  14. Theo: Yes. Refund status is not established by this handoff. Identity, condition, inventory status, and a customer remedy are different questions.
  15. Hana: I'll read the note back: K25 matched, packaging appears unopened, condition review with Mira, no sale release.
  16. Theo: That keeps the stock status accurate. It also avoids claiming that the item is either approved or rejected before the review provides its result.
  17. Hana: So pending isn't the same as rejected, either. We don't have the condition decision yet.
  18. Theo: Exactly. A disposition still needs to follow the actual review process. We should not choose sale, rejection, repair, or another outcome from appearance alone.
  19. Hana: I'll pass on exactly that, with Mira as owner. The next shift will know what remains outstanding.
  20. Theo: Thank you. Review completion is still pending with Mira. K25 is matched to the return, but it has not been marked ready for sale.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Unopened appearance is not release

A returns worker explains K25's status to a picker.

  1. Picker: K25 looks unopened. Could I count it as available stock for the next kettle order?
  2. Returns worker: Returns intake matched the reference, but condition review is pending. It remains on inspection hold.
  3. Picker: So received means it is here, not that someone has approved it for sale again?
  4. Returns worker: Exactly. Mira owns the review and will confirm the disposition before we call it restocked.
  5. Picker: I don't want to label it defective either, since nobody has established a fault.
  6. Returns worker: That is the right distinction. Uninspected is neither confirmed defective nor confirmed suitable for release.
  7. Picker: Can you give me the reference to use when I ask about its availability later?
  8. Returns worker: Use return K25 and ask for the review status, rather than simply asking whether it arrived.
  9. Picker: I'll leave it out of available quantity until that stock status is actually confirmed.
  10. Returns worker: Thanks. The order desk can then distinguish an available unit from a return awaiting a decision.
A returned item differs from its approval

A returns worker queries an item mismatch with the returns desk.

  1. Worker: The authorization lists a black desk lamp, but the returned carton contains a silver reading lamp.
  2. Returns clerk: Could you give me both item codes, rather than relying only on the color descriptions?
  3. Worker: The authorization says DL-B. The item label says RL-S, so both type and suffix differ.
  4. Returns clerk: That is an identity discrepancy. We need to investigate before assigning it the approved return's stock status.
  5. Worker: I'll record what arrived without claiming the customer deliberately sent a different item.
  6. Returns clerk: Please do. A mismatch establishes the difference, not who caused it or why.
  7. Worker: Should the query name the expected and actual item separately so the customer team can compare?
  8. Returns clerk: Yes, include DL-B expected and RL-S received under the return reference you have.
  9. Worker: The item will remain awaiting review; neither restocking nor supplier return has been decided.
  10. Returns clerk: Correct. I'll take the reference check and ask the customer team for the matching order information.
Review completed but disposition still pending

A returns worker checks a confusing status with a returns checker.

  1. Worker: The screen says inspection complete for R88, but disposition still says pending. Are those contradictory?
  2. Checker: No. I completed the condition check, but the next-stock decision belongs to the returns coordinator.
  3. Worker: Then inspection complete doesn't automatically authorize restocking, even when that review stage has finished?
  4. Checker: Correct. Different stages can have different owners. The coordinator still needs to confirm the next status.
  5. Worker: What can I accurately tell the order desk, which is asking whether the unit is available?
  6. Checker: Tell them condition review is complete, disposition pending, and available-stock status has not been confirmed.
  7. Worker: That is clearer than saying the return is done, which could imply everything is settled.
  8. Checker: Exactly. Please use R88 so the coordinator can find this particular review result quickly.
  9. Worker: I'll ask for the disposition update without reopening a condition check you have already completed.
  10. Checker: Thank you. That keeps the remaining question focused on the actual outstanding decision.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Unopened-looking is not ready for sale
  1. Read appears unopened with emphasis on appears.
  2. Repeat the matched return reference and the condition review pending with Mira.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Hand over another pending return review

Returned toaster T36 matches its return reference. Packaging appears unopened, but condition review is pending with Jo. Intake staff cannot release it for sale.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Intake: "The returned item is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. T36

T36 is the toaster reference supplied for this new return.

2. Colleague: "The packaging ___ unopened."

Answer and explanation

C. appears

Appears qualifies the visual observation without proving internal condition or past use.

3. Intake: "The review owner is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Jo

Jo is the named person responsible for the pending assessment.

4. Colleague: "Sale release is not ___."

Answer and explanation

B. authorized

Intake staff lack authority to release the item before the required process.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 07: Returns and stock status
Original fictional case: Returns intake worker Theo receives kettle K25, which matches its return reference. The packaging appears unopened, but the required condition review is still pending. Returns checker Mira owns that review. Theo cannot mark the kettle ready for sale. Colleague Hana needs a clear handoff separating reference matching, apparent packaging condition, review status, and release authority. No refund, functional check, product-safety finding, or saleable-stock decision has been completed in this scenario.
Language workshop: Unopened-looking is not ready for sale
Communication goal: Confirm return identity, qualify an external observation, and refer condition review without releasing the item as saleable.
Language guidance: K25 matches the return reference, and its packaging appears unopened. Neither fact completes the condition review or proves unused, functional, safe, or saleable condition. Mira owns the review, and Theo cannot mark the kettle ready for sale.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is kettle K25. / It matches the return reference. / The packaging appears unopened. / I cannot confirm that the kettle has never been used. / Condition review is still pending. / Mira owns that review. / I cannot mark it ready for sale. / A reference match is not a condition approval. / No functional check is confirmed here. / We have no established product-safety finding from this intake. / This handoff does not confirm a refund. / Keep the review status pending until the required decision. / Please include the observation and the named reviewer. / The item has not been approved as saleable stock. / Mira owning the review does not mean it is finished. / The next step is Mira's condition review.
Vocabulary: return intake: Initial receiving and recording of returned goods. / return reference: Identifier linking goods to a specific return arrangement. / returned item: Product brought back through a return process. / reference match: Correspondence between the item and the return record. / packaging appearance: Visible external state of the packaging. / appears unopened: Looks unopened without proving past use or internal condition. / condition review: Examination required to assess returned-item condition. / returns checker: Person responsible for the relevant return examination. / pending review: Status indicating the required examination is unfinished. / saleable stock: Inventory approved as suitable for sale under the applicable process. / ready for sale: Status indicating required sale-release conditions have been met. / release authority: Permission to approve an item for a particular use or status. / functional check: Examination of whether a product operates as required. / product-safety finding: Established conclusion about relevant safety requirements. / unused condition: State of not having been used, requiring appropriate evidence. / intake observation: Fact or qualified impression recorded during initial receipt. / review owner: Person responsible for completing the pending assessment. / stock status: System or process classification of inventory condition and availability. / restocking: Returning goods to the relevant inventory under the actual process. / disposition: Authorized decision about the next treatment of returned goods. / refund status: Whether a financial return to the customer has been approved or completed. / condition evidence: Information supporting a conclusion about item condition. / review completion: Actual finishing of the required assessment. / status release: Authorized change from a restricted or pending state.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Inventory and shift handoffsThe recount agrees, the cause does not+

Your goal

Transfer a verified count difference, distinguish completed recount from pending investigation, and preserve adjustment authority.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

The recount agrees, the cause does not

At shift change, inventory clerk Priya hands item T9 in B11 to evening clerk Sam for follow-up. The first cycle count found eighteen units; a completed recount also found eighteen. The system still shows twenty. The two-unit difference has no confirmed cause. Sam can review transaction history, but no stock adjustment is approved. Priya must make clear that recounting is already complete while cause investigation and any adjustment decision remain unfinished.

Priya: Outgoing inventory clerk / Sam: Evening inventory clerk

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What do both physical counts show?

Answer and explanation

B. Eighteen units

The first count and completed recount both agree on eighteen units.

2. What does the system show?

Answer and explanation

A. Twenty units

The system balance remains twenty despite the verified physical count of eighteen.

3. What is Sam's next task?

Answer and explanation

B. Review transaction history

The recount is complete, and Sam can review the transaction history for the unexplained difference.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Both counts are eighteen, while the system shows twenty: the verified physical quantity is two units below the recorded balance. The recount is complete. The cause is not confirmed, and no adjustment has been approved.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cycle count
Physical inventory check of selected stock on a recurring basis.perform a cycle count
recount
Second count to verify a previous quantity.confirm the recount
verified count
Quantity supported by the completed checking process.report the verified count
system balance
Quantity currently recorded in the inventory system.compare the system balance
count variance
Difference between counted and system quantities.quantify the count variance
two-unit deficit
Count two units below the reference balance.report a two-unit deficit
transaction history
Record of inventory receipts, issues, transfers, and adjustments.review transaction history
receipt transaction
Record of stock received into the relevant inventory.check the receipt transaction
issue transaction
Record of stock taken out for an order or use.check the issue transaction
transfer transaction
Record of stock moved between locations or inventory categories.review the transfer transaction
stock adjustment
Authorized change to recorded inventory to correct a difference.request a stock adjustment
adjustment approval
Permission to make the relevant inventory correction.await adjustment approval
posting
Recording a transaction in the relevant system, possibly later than physical movement.compare movement and posting times
unposted movement
Movement not yet reflected in the relevant records.investigate a possible unposted movement
audit trail
Record allowing changes and actions to be traced.preserve the audit trail
count sheet
Document recording an inventory count and its context.retain the count sheet
item-location pair
Specific combination of product and storage position.confirm the item-location pair
outgoing clerk
Worker transferring responsibility at the end of a shift.brief the outgoing clerk
incoming clerk
Worker taking responsibility on the next shift.identify the incoming clerk
completed check
Verification step already performed and recorded.distinguish a completed check
pending investigation
Further inquiry whose result is not yet established.hand over the pending investigation
cause finding
Supported conclusion explaining the discrepancy.avoid an unsupported cause finding
handoff acknowledgment
Confirmation that the next person accepts the stated follow-up.obtain a handoff acknowledgment
inventory integrity
Reliability of inventory records and their supporting evidence.protect inventory integrity

Transaction history can help explain a difference, but reviewing it is not the same as finding the cause or posting an adjustment. Preserve the original figures, item-location pair, and supporting record instead of overwriting the system merely to make it agree.

C Notice the language

Do not hand over completed work as if it were still missing

A useful handoff states what has already been verified as well as what remains open. Repeating the count may not answer a transaction-history question. Preserve both figures and the completed recount without implying that an agreed count gives automatic authority to adjust stock.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the item and location
This handoff concerns T9 in B11.
Give the first count
The first count found eighteen units.
Give the recount result
The completed recount also found eighteen.
Give the system figure
The system still shows twenty.
Quantify the difference
The count is two units below the system balance.
Keep the cause open
No cause has been confirmed.
Name completed work
The recount is complete, not still pending.
Name the next check
Please review the transaction history.
Accept ownership
I will take the transaction review.
Preserve the authority limit
No stock adjustment is approved.
Avoid an invented explanation
An unposted movement is a possibility to check, not a finding.
Avoid an accusation
The difference does not by itself prove theft.
Preserve the evidence
Keep both counts and the system figure in the record.
Separate acknowledgment and outcome
Taking the handoff does not resolve the variance.
Request a read-back
Please repeat the figures and the remaining task.
Close with the current status
Eighteen verified, twenty recorded, cause unknown, adjustment unapproved.
6 language and precision notes
Also
Shows the recount agrees with the original eighteen-unit result.
Still
Indicates the system remains at twenty rather than claiming an adjustment occurred.
Below versus above
Gives the direction of the two-unit variance.
Completed versus pending
Applies to separate tasks: recount complete, investigation pending.
Possible versus found
A suggested explanation is not a verified cause.
Will review
Assigns future work without implying review completion or adjustment approval.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handoff is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. T9 in B11: eighteen counted and recounted, twenty recorded, cause unknown.

The accurate message preserves both verified counts, the unchanged balance, and uncertainty about cause.

2. Which task is already complete?

Answer and explanation

B. The recount that also found eighteen.

The briefing explicitly states that the recount is complete and agrees at eighteen.

3. What does the count variance establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Two fewer physical units than the system balance.

The two-unit difference is arithmetic evidence, not proof of cause or authority.

4. Which next statement respects the approval limit?

Answer and explanation

B. I will review history; no adjustment is approved yet.

Reviewing records and accepting ownership do not authorize an inventory adjustment.

E Conversations

The recount agrees, the cause does not

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Priya: Outgoing inventory clerk / Sam: Evening inventory clerk

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Adjustment approval
  • pending investigation
  • system balance
  • audit trail
  • transaction history
  • recount
  • verified count
  • count variance
  • handoff acknowledgment
  • item-location pair
  1. Priya
    Sam, I've got one count issue for handover: T9 in B11. Eighteen on the first count, eighteen on the recount, twenty in the system.
  2. Sam
    Is the 1 finished? I want to be sure I'm taking an investigation, not a request for a second count.
  3. Priya
    Finished, and it agreed at eighteen. The note needs both results, otherwise the next shift could think we're still waiting for that check.
  4. Sam
    I'll retain the 2 alongside twenty in the system. Have you established why they differ?
  5. Priya
    No. A movement or transaction might explain it, but I haven't checked enough to say that one does.
  6. Sam
    I'll take the 3 review. We need the actual records before turning a possible explanation into a finding.
  7. Priya
    Also, no adjustment is approved. Two matching counts don't mean I had authority to replace the recorded twenty with eighteen.
  8. Sam
    Yes, 4 is separate. Taking this handoff doesn't give me permission to post a change either.
  9. Priya
    Please keep B11 in the note. We haven't counted every location holding T9, so this isn't a total-stock statement.
  10. Sam
    I'll keep the 5 explicit. T9 without B11 could send someone to the wrong balance.
  11. Priya
    And say two below the recorded balance, not two remaining. There are eighteen physically here, not two.
  12. Sam
    Agreed. The 6 is minus two units; the physical count is eighteen. I'll use both figures with their labels.
  13. Priya
    The cause stays unknown for now. I don't have evidence of theft or a particular person making an error.
  14. Sam
    That's important for the 7. Keep the original figures and count results so we can trace what happened.
  15. Priya
    Can I name you for the transaction review in my handover? I'd like the next person to know who has that follow-up.
  16. Sam
    Yes. My 8 covers that review. Mark it assigned to me, with the cause still unresolved.
  17. Priya
    Thanks. Read the current position back once, please, including the part that's already done and the part you're taking.
  18. Sam
    T9, B11: eighteen counted and recounted; 9 twenty; cause unknown; adjustment unapproved. Recount complete, history review with Sam.
  19. Priya
    That's it. The physical check is complete, but the reason for the difference and any authorized correction are still outstanding.
  20. Sam
    I'll continue the 10 from that point. I'll preserve the evidence rather than make the record agree before the explanation is established.

Answers and explanations
  1. recount Turn 2. The recount has already been completed and agrees at eighteen, so it is not the missing next action.
  2. verified count Turn 4. Eighteen is supported by the first count and recount, while the recorded balance remains twenty.
  3. transaction history Turn 6. The history is the next evidence source to review; it has not yet established a cause for the difference.
  4. Adjustment approval Turn 8. Approval to change the inventory record remains separate from verifying the physical count or accepting the review.
  5. item-location pair Turn 10. T9 and B11 together define the count being discussed; the finding does not cover all locations of T9.
  6. count variance Turn 12. Eighteen counted minus twenty recorded gives a variance of minus two units, not a physical total of two.
  7. audit trail Turn 14. Retaining both counts and the original system figure keeps the evidence traceable instead of replacing it with an assumed cause.
  8. handoff acknowledgment Turn 16. Sam accepts responsibility for the history review, not a claim that it is already complete or the discrepancy resolved.
  9. system balance Turn 18. The system balance is still twenty because no authorized adjustment has changed the recorded figure.
  10. pending investigation Turn 20. The remaining investigation concerns the unexplained difference and transaction history, not a recount already completed.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Priya: Sam, I've got one count issue for handover: T9 in B11. Eighteen on the first count, eighteen on the recount, twenty in the system.
  2. Sam: Is the recount finished? I want to be sure I'm taking an investigation, not a request for a second count.
  3. Priya: Finished, and it agreed at eighteen. The note needs both results, otherwise the next shift could think we're still waiting for that check.
  4. Sam: I'll retain the verified count alongside twenty in the system. Have you established why they differ?
  5. Priya: No. A movement or transaction might explain it, but I haven't checked enough to say that one does.
  6. Sam: I'll take the transaction history review. We need the actual records before turning a possible explanation into a finding.
  7. Priya: Also, no adjustment is approved. Two matching counts don't mean I had authority to replace the recorded twenty with eighteen.
  8. Sam: Yes, Adjustment approval is separate. Taking this handoff doesn't give me permission to post a change either.
  9. Priya: Please keep B11 in the note. We haven't counted every location holding T9, so this isn't a total-stock statement.
  10. Sam: I'll keep the item-location pair explicit. T9 without B11 could send someone to the wrong balance.
  11. Priya: And say two below the recorded balance, not two remaining. There are eighteen physically here, not two.
  12. Sam: Agreed. The count variance is minus two units; the physical count is eighteen. I'll use both figures with their labels.
  13. Priya: The cause stays unknown for now. I don't have evidence of theft or a particular person making an error.
  14. Sam: That's important for the audit trail. Keep the original figures and count results so we can trace what happened.
  15. Priya: Can I name you for the transaction review in my handover? I'd like the next person to know who has that follow-up.
  16. Sam: Yes. My handoff acknowledgment covers that review. Mark it assigned to me, with the cause still unresolved.
  17. Priya: Thanks. Read the current position back once, please, including the part that's already done and the part you're taking.
  18. Sam: T9, B11: eighteen counted and recounted; system balance twenty; cause unknown; adjustment unapproved. Recount complete, history review with Sam.
  19. Priya: That's it. The physical check is complete, but the reason for the difference and any authorized correction are still outstanding.
  20. Sam: I'll continue the pending investigation from that point. I'll preserve the evidence rather than make the record agree before the explanation is established.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two counts agree, the system differs

An inventory worker hands T9 in B11 to evening clerk Sam.

  1. Worker: The cycle count found eighteen T9 units in B11 twice; the system still shows twenty.
  2. Evening clerk: Then the recount confirms a two-unit variance. Do we know what caused the difference?
  3. Worker: No cause is confirmed, and no inventory adjustment has been approved against this count.
  4. Evening clerk: I'll take the transaction-history review. Did either count include stock in another location?
  5. Worker: No. Both counts covered B11 only; I haven't made a claim about other locations.
  6. Evening clerk: That scope matters. I'll compare the relevant movements without treating a possible transfer as established.
  7. Worker: Please acknowledge the discrepancy in the shift handoff so it doesn't disappear when our shift ends.
  8. Evening clerk: I have it: T9, B11, physical eighteen, recorded twenty, cause unknown, adjustment unapproved.
  9. Worker: Thank you. Let the next shift know the recount is complete but the investigation is not.
  10. Evening clerk: I'll keep those stages separate and report the review outcome against this count reference.
A movement after the count

An associate clarifies an apparent inventory contradiction with a clerk.

  1. Associate: I counted ten at nine o'clock, but your screen now shows eight. Was my count rejected?
  2. Inventory clerk: Not necessarily. The transaction history shows a two-unit pick at nine fifteen from that location.
  3. Associate: Then the figures may describe different times rather than two disagreeing counts of the same moment.
  4. Inventory clerk: Yes, though we should confirm that the pick relates to the exact item and bin.
  5. Associate: The count was item PN-4 in C16. Those are the references I used this morning.
  6. Inventory clerk: The recorded pick matches both. Your ten preceded the two-unit movement, producing the later eight.
  7. Associate: That explains the timing. Please don't label the earlier count inaccurate merely because the current quantity changed.
  8. Inventory clerk: I won't. I'll identify the count time and the later transaction in the review note.
  9. Associate: Does comparing these two different-time figures justify an inventory adjustment?
  10. Inventory clerk: No. The recorded pick explains the change; this is not a confirmed inventory variance.
The handoff recipient cannot take the task

A picker and outgoing clerk find the actual owner of an unresolved order.

  1. Picker: Order F42 still has a three-unit short pick. Are you taking the follow-up after our shift?
  2. Clerk: I'm leaving too, so I can't be its evening owner. We need to name the incoming contact.
  3. Picker: A handoff without a named owner could leave this unresolved.
  4. Clerk: The evening pick desk is staffed by Lena. She has confirmed she's taking unresolved picking queries.
  5. Picker: Then the handoff should name Lena and state three units outstanding, not the entire order missing.
  6. Clerk: Yes. The other seven units are confirmed picked, and the remaining allocation check is still pending.
  7. Picker: Please give Lena the order, quantities, and cutoff, and request acknowledgment.
  8. Clerk: I can contact her now and ask for acknowledgment, rather than only adding an unnamed note.
  9. Picker: I'll keep the status as seven picked, three outstanding until her desk reports a confirmed change.
  10. Clerk: Lena has acknowledged ownership of the allocation check. The quantities remain unchanged.

Two scenarios to practice

The recount agrees, the cause does not
  1. Read eighteen counted, eighteen recounted, and twenty recorded.
  2. Repeat minus two as the variance, not the physical quantity.

Hand over another verified count difference

Item U8 in D03 was counted at twenty-seven units, and the completed recount agrees. The system shows thirty. The cause is unknown. Clerk Jo accepts transaction-history review; no adjustment is approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Clerk: "Both counts give ___ units."

Answer and explanation

C. twenty-seven

Twenty-seven is the verified physical quantity supported by the completed recount.

2. Colleague: "The system balance is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. thirty

Thirty remains the recorded amount because no adjustment has been approved.

3. Clerk: "The physical count is ___ units below the system."

Answer and explanation

B. three

Thirty recorded minus twenty-seven counted gives a three-unit deficit.

4. Colleague: "The transaction-history review belongs to ___."

Answer and explanation

A. Jo

Jo accepts the pending review without implying its completion or approving an adjustment.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 08: Inventory and shift handoffs
Original fictional case: At shift change, inventory clerk Priya hands item T9 in B11 to evening clerk Sam for follow-up. The first cycle count found eighteen units; a completed recount also found eighteen. The system still shows twenty. The two-unit difference has no confirmed cause. Sam can review transaction history, but no stock adjustment is approved. Priya must make clear that recounting is already complete while cause investigation and any adjustment decision remain unfinished.
Language workshop: The recount agrees, the cause does not
Communication goal: Transfer a verified count difference, distinguish completed recount from pending investigation, and preserve adjustment authority.
Language guidance: Both counts are eighteen, while the system shows twenty: the verified physical quantity is two units below the recorded balance. The recount is complete. The cause is not confirmed, and no adjustment has been approved.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This handoff concerns T9 in B11. / The first count found eighteen units. / The completed recount also found eighteen. / The system still shows twenty. / The count is two units below the system balance. / No cause has been confirmed. / The recount is complete, not still pending. / Please review the transaction history. / I will take the transaction review. / No stock adjustment is approved. / An unposted movement is a possibility to check, not a finding. / The difference does not by itself prove theft. / Keep both counts and the system figure in the record. / Taking the handoff does not resolve the variance. / Please repeat the figures and the remaining task. / Eighteen verified, twenty recorded, cause unknown, adjustment unapproved.
Vocabulary: cycle count: Physical inventory check of selected stock on a recurring basis. / recount: Second count to verify a previous quantity. / verified count: Quantity supported by the completed checking process. / system balance: Quantity currently recorded in the inventory system. / count variance: Difference between counted and system quantities. / two-unit deficit: Count two units below the reference balance. / transaction history: Record of inventory receipts, issues, transfers, and adjustments. / receipt transaction: Record of stock received into the relevant inventory. / issue transaction: Record of stock taken out for an order or use. / transfer transaction: Record of stock moved between locations or inventory categories. / stock adjustment: Authorized change to recorded inventory to correct a difference. / adjustment approval: Permission to make the relevant inventory correction. / posting: Recording a transaction in the relevant system, possibly later than physical movement. / unposted movement: Movement not yet reflected in the relevant records. / audit trail: Record allowing changes and actions to be traced. / count sheet: Document recording an inventory count and its context. / item-location pair: Specific combination of product and storage position. / outgoing clerk: Worker transferring responsibility at the end of a shift. / incoming clerk: Worker taking responsibility on the next shift. / completed check: Verification step already performed and recorded. / pending investigation: Further inquiry whose result is not yet established. / cause finding: Supported conclusion explaining the discrepancy. / handoff acknowledgment: Confirmation that the next person accepts the stated follow-up. / inventory integrity: Reliability of inventory records and their supporting evidence.
Speaking task: Read eighteen counted, eighteen recounted, and twenty recorded. Repeat minus two as the variance, not the physical quantity. Read Sam's next action while keeping the recount complete and adjustment unapproved.
Partner: Priya: Outgoing inventory clerk / Sam: Evening inventory clerk
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Item U8 in D03 was counted at twenty-seven units, and the completed recount agrees. The system shows thirty. The cause is unknown. Clerk Jo accepts transaction-history review; no adjustment is approved.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
